# Changelog

All notable changes to this project, generated from `changes/` entries on each version bump. Newest first.























































































































## 2026-08-31 — mymatasan 1.148.1 (6310d1c)

### Changed

- **mymatasan**: Redesigned the Case files screen, which had rendered into `.panel`/`.panel-head` classes with no CSS rules anywhere in the stylesheet and so looked unstyled. It is now a proper `.workspace cases-page` screen with a dismissible four-step guide ('How a case works': open a case, add the evidence, the footage is held, export then close) that deep-links into the matching manual article anchors, a clickable progress rail on the open case (Opened / Evidence / Write-up / Outcome, pressing Outcome opens the close dialog), a four-tile stat strip (Evidence, Clips held, clips only still present because the case is holding them, footage already gone), a search box and Open/Closed/All filter with counts, and card rows with assignee initials. Empty states now name the screen the evidence came from (Timeline, Objects, Notifications, Add note) instead of a generic 'nothing here'. Along with the redesign, six real bugs shipped in the old screen were fixed: the evidence row's Play button called a callback the app never passed in and silently did nothing - there is now an in-page player that fetches the segment and seeks to the marked moment; the case dialogs inherited the video player's hardcoded dark background, so light-theme text was near-black on near-black with no padding; a global `button.active` style made the selected list row's own title invisible; the pending Outcome step drew a checkmark next to 'Still open'; the footage-hold panel showed a reassuring green message on a case holding zero clips; and switching cases kept the previous case's already-built export bundle on screen instead of clearing it. `tools/fleetbench/uicheck_cases.js` was updated to match the new markup (`.cases-page` instead of `.cases-panel`, and the primary action button instead of the first button in the row, which is now Cancel).

### Fixed

- **mymatasan**: The bounding box burned into a face alert's snapshot said the raw model class 'Face' even on a recognized person, while the notification row right next to the picture named them - and the picture is the copy that actually leaves the appliance: the notification image, the AI Log zoom lightbox, 'Download with box', and every outbound destination (webhook, MQTT, Telegram, email). A new `faceBoxLabel` in `apps/mymatasan/services/notification.go` makes `alertBoxLabel` return the person exactly as enrolled - not title-cased, since that turns a name like 'van der Berg' into 'Van Der Berg' - or the fixed label 'Unknown face' for a stranger, on every face alert. In the multi-box (crowd-style) annotation path the identity is applied only when there is exactly one box, since a `MetaBox` carries a class label and confidence but never a person, and stamping one name across several boxes would misattribute the others. The AI Log's client-side detail overlay (`AlertDetailModal` in vision.js) drew the same class-name label with the identical bug and now mirrors the fix, with a new `.alert-modal-bb-label.is-identity` style that drops the class-name capitalization for a person's name. New i18n key `vi.unknownFace` added to en/ms/zh/ar for the client-side label.
## 2026-08-31 — myiotsan 0.31.1, mymatasan 1.147.1, myseliasan 1.80.1, core 1.111.1 (439beb5)

### Fixed

- **shared,mymatasan,myseliasan,myiotsan**: Three fixes to shipped behaviour, all verified by driving the real screens rather than by unit tests. (1) A face-recognition alert now says WHO. The recognized identity lived only in alert.Label, which the notification body prints only when a destination's 'label' field is enabled - with it off, a match delivered 'Lobby Entrance - 94% confidence' and named nobody. The structured Data map that webhook/MQTT/Telegram/email consumers actually read never carried the identity at all, on any destination. apps/mymatasan/services/notification.go's renderVisionAlert now adds a person line to the body when the label is suppressed and always promotes person/personId/recognized/faceConfidence into Data; recognized:false is always sent so a consumer can route on strangers, and an unrecognized face is never given a name. alertTemplateContext gained the {{person}}, {{recognized}} and {{faceConfidence}} custom-field tokens - {{person}} was DOCUMENTED in infra/vision/object.go and never implemented, so every custom field using it silently expanded to nothing. (2) The in-app Notifications page showed neither the person nor the plate. Its AI row never renders the notification body; it builds its own field list, and 'objectLabel' there is the RAW worker label, which deliberately wins over alert.Label - so a recognized person rendered as 'Object: face' and a read plate as 'Object: license plate'. notifications.js now reads the identity the detector promotes into the alert's metadata and shows Person (face) and Plate (LPR). On a face alert the generic Confidence is replaced by a Match score: the stored alert confidence is max(matchScore, 0.5), so it was a floored duplicate on a recognized face and a hard-coded 50% presented as measured on an unrecognized one. A stranger is labelled 'Unknown person' rather than omitted. Four notif.* keys added across en/ms/zh/ar. (3) Refreshing the page threw the operator back to the dashboard in mymatasan, myseliasan, myiotsan and mypintusan. New frontend/shared/src/stickyTab.js (useStickyTab/read/write/clearStickyTab) remembers the active section in sessionStorage - per tab, never sent to the server, and it survives reload and restore-after-crash. It takes the set of sections the signed-in user may reach, so a section that was renamed by an upgrade, or one an administrator left behind before a viewer signed in on the same tab, falls back to the landing page and the stale value is rewritten rather than bouncing again; sign-out forgets the section so the next person does not land inside the last operator's work. In mymatasan the actual culprit was separate: enterApp forced the dashboard on entry and the session-restore path ran through it, so even a section every role can reach did not survive - it now takes a keepSection option that a refresh sets and a fresh sign-in does not.
## 2026-08-31 — mymatasan 1.147.0, core 1.111.0 (17d196d)

### Changed

- **domain,mymatasan**: mymatasan's sign-in moved from replaying HTTP Basic on every request to a one-time credential exchange: apps/mymatasan/app/wire_routes.go now mounts the shared PUBLIC POST /api/auth/login and POST /api/auth/logout (apps/mymatasan/apis/local_auth.go's new NewLocalLoginApi binding, the same endpoints myiotsan and mypintusan already used), and the SPA (App.js) no longer holds the password in memory or replays a Basic header per request - it signs in once, probes GET /api/auth/session on boot to restore the session across a page reload (previously a refresh looked exactly like a sign-out), and calls logout on sign-out. This also drops a bcrypt verification from every request, which is what previously capped mymatasan's HTTP throughput. HTTP Basic remains fully supported server-side for scripts/API clients and media tiles. Separately, the shared appliance session cookie (domain/shared/apis/local_auth.go, used by mymatasan, myiotsan, and mypintusan) now SLIDES: a cookie-authenticated request past half its 12h TTL re-issues the cookie, so an active cookie-only session is continuously extended instead of hitting a fixed 12h wall from sign-in regardless of activity. Finally, mymatasan's People screen (faces.js, frontend-only) got two independent fixes bundled into this same session: the enrolment dialog now shows upload progress and disables every way to exit (X/Close/Esc/backdrop/drop-zone/mode-toggle/per-photo-delete) plus a beforeunload guard while photos are uploading, and no longer aborts a whole batch on one unreadable file; and the 'Enrolled people' roster gained search, state filter chips with counts, a sort, a list-vs-cards view toggle (persisted, defaults by roster size), sticky A-Z dividers, and a Show more/Show all pager so it scales to hundreds of enrolled people, plus a max-height/scroll on the per-camera watchlist picker. 26 new faces.* i18n keys plus app.invalidCredentials were added across en/ms/zh/ar.
## 2026-08-30 — myidsan 1.50.2, myiotsan 0.31.0, mymatasan 1.146.2, myseliasan 1.80.0, core 1.110.0 (e9a3071)

### Added

- **myidsan**: The sign-in server now records which applications an account was actually let into. Until now its history showed that somebody signed in and stopped there — which is only half the story, because signing in happens once and the access it is then traded for happens separately for every application. If an account were ever misused, the question you would need answered is which systems it reached, and this was the one component that knew and did not write it down. Refusals are recorded too, and are arguably the more useful half: an attempt to have somebody's sign-in sent to an address that was never registered, a request from an application nobody has registered, or a one-time code being used a second time are exactly what an attack on this looks like, and a history containing only successes cannot show one.
- **infra**: Gave mypintusan an ADMINISTRATIVE TRAIL: an append-only record of who changed the rules about who gets in. Item 7 and the last item of the mypintusan hardening register, and the only one on it that was a FEATURE GAP rather than a defect - there was nothing to bench until the trail existed, so the feature and its 66-check live bench landed together. THE GAP. This app keeps the best log in the suite: entities.AccessEvent records every badge presented at every door - who, where, when, granted or denied, and why. What it never recorded is WHO DECIDED THEY COULD. A grant created at 23:40, a holiday deleted the morning of a shutdown, a perimeter door's offline policy flipped from deny to cached, a badge quietly issued to a holder nobody added to the roster: every one of those changes what the access log will say tomorrow, and none of them left a mark that survives. The gap was sharper here than the identical gap was in mymatasan BECAUSE THE ACCESS LOG HIDES IT - a grant edit does not look like an incident, it looks like ordinary green badge rows three weeks later, on a door the person was never meant to reach, with `decision: granted, reason: ok` beside them. The log answers 'did this happen' perfectly and 'was this supposed to happen' not at all. Built on domain/shared/audit, the same append-only table myidsan, myseliasan and mymatasan already use, so bcrypt-grade discipline about field caps, hostile input and retention is one implementation rather than five: new services/audit.go (the action vocabulary and target types), apis/audit.go (GET /api/audit + GET /api/audit.csv, the request-scoped Auditor, and the middleware below), services/metrics.go, app/audit_retention.go. Instrumented: grant create/delete, group create/delete, group membership add/remove, schedule create/delete, holiday create/delete, holder create, credential issue/revoke, door create, remote unlock, lockdown set (BOTH directions - a site sealed during an incident and quietly reopened twenty minutes later is the sequence nobody can reconstruct, because lifting a lockdown produces no event at all, it just makes the denials stop), settings save/reset, and user create/update/delete/password-reset. THE DESIGN DECISION THAT MATTERS, and the reason this is not just a list of Record() calls: the default is RECORDED. apis.NewAuditMiddleware records any ACCEPTED mutating request that reached no handler which audited itself, as `api.write`. A trail instrumented handler-by-handler has this app's own recurring failure mode built into it - seven benches found the same shape seven times, THE MECHANISM IS RIGHT AND NOTHING REACHES IT - and here the symptom would be an empty result in an investigation years later, by which time nobody can tell an appliance where nothing happened from one where the recording was never wired. So a handler's own entry is an ENRICHMENT rather than the only chance, and a route added next year is in the trail on the day it ships. Proven live: POST /api/setup/complete is served by shared code this app does not own, no handler here audits it, and it is in the trail. THE ENTRIES ARE SENTENCES, NOT IDS. A schedule entry carries the HOURS ('Mon 22:00-06:00') because 'schedule created' tells an auditor nothing while the window is what shows somebody widened the night shift to cover 02:00; a grant entry names the group, the door AND the schedule in one line; door.create records the offline policy, the cache TTL and the Secure Channel requirement, because there is no PUT /api/doors and those three values decide what the door does on the day the network is gone; a settings save DIFFS the live values against the save and names only what moved (the screen posts the whole object every time, so the request body cannot answer 'what changed'); a revocation carries the REASON, which is what turns a card presented on Saturday from a denial into an incident. NO SECRET EVER: a rekey is recorded as the fact that a key was replaced, never the key - the trail is readable by every administrator and exported to CSV, and a site base key in it is the exact secret Secure Channel exists to protect. The bench asserts that over EVERY row, plus the absence of passwords and bcrypt hashes. Authorization: /api/audit is admin-only, and /api/audit.csv IS ITS OWN CATALOG RULE - paths match segment-wise, so 'audit.csv' is not governed by 'audit', and a rule covering the listing but not the export would leave the whole trail downloadable by a rule nobody wrote (#224's shape exactly). New viewAudit capability drives a new Admin trail section in the rail, hidden for operator and viewer by the same matrix that refuses the request. New Trail.js screen with filter chips, a CSV export link and localisation in all four languages. New tests: apis/audit_test.go (the middleware's guarantee, no double-recording, reads ignored, the settings diff, and that the trail offers NO write route) and apis/audit_actions_test.go, which reads the source and fails the build if a declared action constant loses its call site - the grep that already found five dead audit constants on myidsan and three unreachable alarms on this app, turned into a build failure, because an absent record and a record of absence look identical in a query. WHAT THE BENCH FOUND: the CSV needed a UTF-8 BOM or Excel on Windows renders every em dash in an auditor's copy as mojibake; and OPENING THE ARABIC PNG showed `card issued to "Screen Bench Person"` rendering as `"card issued to "Screen Bench Person` - the RTL paragraph direction reordering the quotes of a sentence that is deliberately NOT translated (an audit record says what it said; re-rendering it in the reader's language would make the trail's text depend on who is looking), fixed with <bdi dir="ltr">. Every check passed while that was on screen. STATED RATHER THAN GLOSSED: the middleware runs INSIDE the permission middleware, so a matrix-level 403 is not in the trail (it is in api_log); the branch that records a denial is a handler's own 'administrators only' gate, and in this app's current catalog no role can reach such a route, so that branch is covered by unit test and not on the wire - the bench asserts the true statement instead of a green that means nothing. 66/66 live checks (tools/fleetbench/bench_pintusan_trail.py) and 166/166 on the screen bench with the new section in it.
- **mymatasan**: Rebuilt the People (face enrolment) screen, and fixed the reason it never worked. THE FUNCTIONAL BUG: every request the page made was built as `${origin}/faces` with no `/api` prefix - the one screen in the app that had drifted from the `${apiBase()}/api/...` shape every other screen uses. The SPA's catch-all answers those paths with index.html and a 200, so the roster parsed an HTML page as its person list and rendered an empty, contented-looking screen, and 'Add person' reported success while creating nobody. The screen looked like it worked and did nothing at all. The page's fetch helper now throws on a 200 that is not JSON, so the same drift cannot come back silently. THE DESIGN: the page had NO stylesheet - it was written against a `.page`/`.page-head` vocabulary that has never existed in app.css, so every element fell back to browser defaults: bulleted lists, OS checkboxes and a bare file input. It now uses the app's own furniture (.workspace, .settings-panel, .toolbar) with a person-card roster, and the switch from the Dashboard's anomaly card moved into components/ui.js so both screens share one Toggle instead of two. THE MISSING HALF OF THE FEATURE: adding a person wrote a name and stopped. A person with no faceprints is skipped by the gallery export, so they are recognized by nothing - the screen now says that on the card, and adding somebody opens their enrolment dialog immediately. That dialog offers the two ways to supply a face side by side - files from this computer (picker or drag-and-drop) and a shot from this computer's own camera via getUserMedia - and lists the already-enrolled photos with per-photo delete, which finally calls the GET /api/faces/{id}/embeddings and DELETE /api/faces/embeddings/{id} routes the old screen never touched. Camera capture states the truth about secure contexts: browsers only hand over a camera on HTTPS or localhost, so on a plain http:// LAN address the pane says so instead of reporting a broken webcam. Server side: FaceEmbedding gained a Thumbnail column (the cropped face the model looked at, not the supplied photo) so enrolled shots can be seen and judged, and GET /api/faces now returns each person's faceprint count. Live-verified on a throwaway instance driven over CDP with Chrome's fake camera device: roster counts, camera rules created and deleted on the wire, a pause written through, a faceprint deleted, the capture path posting and the server's own rejection landing in the dialog - in English and Arabic (RTL), light and dark, with an empty console.
- **mymatasan**: Face recognition can now be set up from the app, with a button. THE DEAD END: enrolling a photo on a host without the models failed with 'face models are not installed - run the face-recognition setup to download them'. Every word of that was true and it was useless: the setup it named was ai/setup.ps1 -Faces, a PowerShell script in the source tree, which is not a thing to tell somebody standing in a browser trying to enrol a face. AN ERROR WITHOUT A ROUTE TO THE FIX IS A HALF-BUILT FEATURE. New services/face_models_install.go (FaceModelsInstaller) + three routes on the faces API: GET /api/faces/models says what the host still needs, POST /api/faces/models/install starts a background job, and /install/status streams its log - the same shape as the ffmpeg and AI-runtime installers. It fetches ONLY what is missing (the common real state is 'detector present, recognizer missing', because the export face-blur already uses YuNet), pip-installs opencv-python when the detector's interpreter lacks the FaceDetectorYN/FaceRecognizerSF classes - a host can have both models and still fail, which is a DIFFERENT repair and the status says which one is needed - and then LOADS both models with the real cv2 calls the worker makes, because a file of the right size is not proof it is the right file. The directory is checked writable BEFORE the download: 37 MB is a long way to travel to learn the destination is read-only. checkFaceModelFile rejects the two ways this download succeeds and is still not a model, both arriving as a valid 200: a git-LFS pointer (a ~130-byte text stub GitHub serves for LFS-tracked files through some URL forms - named explicitly, because 'too small' would send somebody to look at their network instead of the URL) and a truncated transfer. The UI is one component in two places: a panel on the People screen that appears the moment the page loads, so the operator is told BEFORE picking a photo rather than by a failure after, and the SAME control in Settings > AI beside the AI-runtime installer, where it also shows the ready state. A rejected enrolment re-checks the host, so the message and the button that fixes it arrive together. The page's live-state line is SUPPRESSED while setup is outstanding - a green 'Recognizing 1 person on 2 cameras' under a banner saying the models are missing is the screen contradicting itself. ALSO FIXED, found by the bench data rather than by reading: rebuildGallery exported a zero-length faceprint straight to the worker, and the worker builds one matrix per person with np.asarray(embeddings), which raises on a ragged list inside a try that wraps the WHOLE gallery load - so ONE malformed row did not degrade one person, it aborted the load and NOBODY was recognized on ANY camera, with a single line on the worker's stderr as the only symptom. The export now drops empty and odd-length vectors (what a truncated write and a model change respectively leave behind) and says so in the log. Verified live end to end on a throwaway instance: the banner listed exactly the one missing 37 MB file, the button downloaded it (38,696,353 bytes - a real file, not an LFS stub), the banner disappeared, and three photos then enrolled through the REAL file picker at 88% quality with their face crops on screen and three 128-d faceprints in the gallery the worker reads. `.install-output` also got its first stylesheet rule - the AI-runtime installer's pip log had been rendering as an unstyled <pre> that grew without bound.
- **mymatasan**: The People screen can now say what face recognition DOES, and choose what it alerts on. THE GAP: enrolling somebody and switching on a camera produces an alert with a boxed snapshot, an event clip, a notification to every configured destination, and optionally a PTZ recall and a relay — every one of which lands on a DIFFERENT screen. From the People page the feature was indistinguishable from one that does nothing. Three additions. (1) WHAT IT ALERTS ON, per camera: 'Anyone enrolled' (a staff door), 'Only chosen people' (a watchlist, with the names picked inline) or 'Unknown faces' (strangers, a perimeter). All three modes have worked in infra/vision/face.go since face rules shipped and NOTHING in the UI could select between them — stranger detection existed and could not be switched on. The include mode does not save at the moment it is chosen, because the server refuses a watchlist with nobody on it (correctly: it would alert on no one); the row opens the list and commits with the first name ticked, rather than posting something that comes back as an error. (2) LAST SEEN, on each person's card: 'Seen 4 minutes ago on Lobby Entrance', from a new GET /api/faces/sightings that reduces a bounded window of recent face alerts to the latest per person. It reads the alert log rather than keeping a tally — the alert IS the record, and a second counter would be a second thing to keep true. Unrecognized faces are reported separately ('2 unrecognized faces seen recently'), because they belong to nobody and a screen showing only known people would otherwise hide that strangers are being seen. (3) A line saying what a match actually does, shown once a camera is on. WHAT THE BENCH FOUND, both invisible to unit tests: the camera row was a flex ROW holding two blocks, so the camera's on/off switch came to sit ON TOP of the first watchlist checkbox — ticking the first person in a watchlist switched the camera off instead, and the harness only caught it because it asserts document.elementFromPoint before every click; and the base stylesheet's `input, select, textarea { width: 100% }` turned the mode dropdown into a full-width bar that squeezed the camera name to nothing and the checkboxes into large empty boxes.

### Fixed

- **myidsan**: Two real defects were found and fixed in .idbackup disaster recovery by a live bench restoring across two hosts. First, the "Keep both" (merge) mode could not actually run against any real server: every myidsan install seeds the same stock role names and the same bootstrap admin email, both columns unique, so a merge restore of the access or identity section hit that constraint on its very first row and aborted with the database driver's own text surfaced to the operator mid-recovery, having already written whatever came before the collision (the restore has no enclosing transaction). Merge mode now checks every table's natural key (role name, account email, app code/audience, redirect URI, directory name, CA name, an account's MFA factor kind, a recovery-code hash, a security key's credential id, and more) before each insert and skips a collision instead of aborting, counting it in the restore result; the target's own row always wins, and anything that would have hung off a skipped record (a second factor, a redirect URI, an auth config) skips with it. Second, and more serious: restoring a section WITHOUT the section it depends on was silently destructive. Selecting only "MFA" with "Replace what is here" wiped every second factor on the server, found no account mapping to place the backup's factors on (the mapping is only built by restoring the identity section), restored none, and reported success — silently disabling every account's second factor, a fleet-wide lockout under a required-MFA policy. A federation-only restore lost every directory group→role mapping the same way. Confirmed live before fixing. A restore now resolves an unselected parent section (roles by name, accounts by email) against what is already on the target host instead of leaving it unmapped; a row whose parent is in neither the backup's selected sections nor the host still skips, unchanged. A mid-restore failure that does still occur now names the section, lists what was already applied, and tells the operator to re-run in "replace" mode instead of surfacing a bare driver error. Confirmed against a real second myidsan host with its own at-rest key: unseal-on-export / re-seal-on-restore for TOTP secrets and the LDAP bind password remains correct across hosts and a reboot.
- **myidsan**: myidsan had ZERO screen checks - every bench it has ever had was an API bench - so the first one (tools/fleetbench/uicheck_idsan_admin.js) drives all twelve admin sections in headless Chrome, in all four languages, and then drives real workflows and confirms the SERVER changed. It found two real defects, both invisible from the API side. First and worse: a 401 did not return the SPA to the sign-in screen. apiRequest threw on any non-OK response and every page caught it like an ordinary error and rendered its own empty state, so an operator whose session had ended - expired, revoked by an administrator, or ended by themselves from the Users page, which includes their own row - was left sitting in a fully drawn admin console with a small red 'session not active' and the Audit log announcing 'No events match these filters'. The screen was lying, and only the screenshot showed it. api.js now announces a lost session on 401 (and deliberately NOT on 403, which is 'you are signed in and may not do this' and is also how the step-up sentinel arrives), and App.js returns to the sign-in screen; the announcement fires at most once per lost session so a page fanning out parallel requests cannot bounce the operator several times, and a new sign-in re-arms it. Second: the Users page offers 'End all sessions' on every row INCLUDING the operator's own, and the confirmation said 'End every session for this account? They will have to sign in again' - wording that quietly assumed somebody else. Pressing it on your own row signs you out immediately, which is a legitimate emergency action but not what that sentence describes. A separate self-worded confirmation now says so, in all four languages. The check itself also proves, per language, that every section renders with a heading, leaks no untranslated dictionary key, and that every enabled control is HIT-TESTABLE at its own centre (document.elementFromPoint at the control's own midpoint - the test that once found a button which rendered perfectly and could not be pressed, in Arabic only); and it exercises create AND DELETE on Roles, because a green run that never deletes has not covered delete. 21/21 in English, 8/8 ms, 8/8 zh, 9/9 ar against the fix; 19/21 against unfixed main on the same English checks.
- **myidsan**: First live exercise of myidsan's security keys, with a REAL WebAuthn ceremony (tools/fleetbench/uicheck_idsan_webauthn.js). WebAuthn is the strongest second factor this server offers and the only part of it nothing had ever driven - a FIDO2 ceremony needs a browser and an authenticator, so its unit tests could verify the service and nothing could register a credential, sign in with it, or remove it. Chrome's DevTools WebAuthn domain supplies a virtual CTAP2 authenticator: real key material, real signatures, real client-data hashing, so navigator.credentials runs the genuine ceremony against a genuine attestation and the app's own client code (lib/webauthn.js) is what drives it. The bench found one real defect: the two-factor card on the Profile page announced 'Your account is protected by a password only. Add an authenticator app for a second factor.' whenever TOTP was off, knowing nothing about security keys - so a user who had just enrolled a FIDO2 key was told, on the security page, that they had no second factor. That is a false statement about an account's protection, on the page whose whole job is reporting it, and the kind an operator auditing accounts would act on. The screenshot put the enrolled key and that sentence side by side. WebAuthnKeys now reports its key count to the sibling card, which says something true in either case, in all four languages. Everything else about the surface held up under real use and is now proven rather than assumed: enrolment through the app's own UI; the enrolment recorded in the audit trail (which matters because removing a factor deletes its own evidence); an account whose ONLY factor is a security key still being CHALLENGED at sign-in rather than waved through - the exact MFA-bypass shape the server-rendered login page once had when its challenger was wired for TOTP alone; the key completing that challenge and signing the user in; rename; and removal demanding the password, refusing a wrong one, and being throttled after eight attempts (401 x8 then 429) rather than being the unmetered password oracle it was before PR #206 - now confirmed live from a browser rather than from a test. 19/19 against the fix, 18/19 against unfixed main. Directory/LDAP, the other half of this work item, already has live coverage (infra/login/ldap_integration_test.go against a real OpenLDAP, RUN_LDAP_IT=1) and is unchanged here.
- **myiotsan**: First live exercise of myiotsan's actuation chokepoint, against a real device on the real embedded broker (tools/fleetbench/iotsan_harness.py + bench_iotsan_actuation.py). myiotsan is the app that WRITES to the physical world and it had never been run against anything - 11 test files, zero live exercise. Its whole safety story is one function, CommandService.Issue, and six ordered gates behind it: read-only by default, admin only, only-what-the-profile-declares, server-side bounds, a per-device duty cycle, and an audit row for every attempt INCLUDING every refusal. That is a claim about the whole program, and no unit test can check it - each caller's own test can pass while a caller nobody wrote a test for reaches the wire another way. So the bench enumerates every path that can move a device and drives each for real: the direct API, a scene, a schedule (test-fired AND fired by the clock), a flow's command node, a flow's mqtt_out node, and the rule engine. A paho client authenticated as the real provisioned device, confined by the real broker ACL, sits on that device's command topic throughout, so 'refused' means the API said no AND nothing arrived on the wire, and every negative assertion is conditioned on a positive that ran first on the same wire. It found one, and it is the one that mattered: the flow canvas's mqtt_out node publishes through broker.Publish - the server's OWN handle, subject to no ACL. Aimed at a device's command topic it moved a relay whose actuation was switched OFF, with a value outside the declared safe range, past the duty-cycle limit, and left nothing in the trail. Four gates bypassed at once, by the one node in the palette whose stated job is bridging a value out - and profile_command.go says outright that no such escape hatch exists, because one would be a remote shell for the building's electrics. A topic a real device would act on as a command is now RESERVED to the guarded path: refused when the flow is saved (naming the topic, the command and the device, and pointing at the command output instead) and refused again at run time, which is the authority and covers a flow drawn before this existed. The refused attempt is recorded in that device's command history like any other refusal, naming the flow as the actor. Any other topic still publishes - the bench checks that the bridge the node exists for still works. Everything else held up and is now proven rather than assumed: all six gates refuse for the right reason and nothing reaches the wire when they do; a refusal is still an audit row naming the actor; the bounds are a range, not a blanket no (the declared max is accepted and lands); an operator and a viewer are both refused actuation and can both still read the trail; scenes, schedules and flow command nodes all fan out through the same chokepoint and are refused with it; a schedule fired by the CLOCK is attributed to 'schedule:<name>' rather than reading 'System'; and the rule engine actuates nothing - rules alert. 45/45 against the fix, 41/45 against unfixed main.
- **myiotsan**: First live exercise of myiotsan's Modbus WRITE path, against tools/sunspec-sim (tools/fleetbench/bench_iotsan_modbus.py). Commanding a polled device - a solar inverter, a battery - ends in modbus.WriteConfirm, which writes once, reads the register back, and never re-issues. That is the right design and it is also self-certifying: a write that went to the wrong register, the wrong unit, or with the wrong sign confirms itself exactly as happily as a correct one, because it reads back whatever it wrote wherever it wrote it. So the bench brings its own Modbus TCP client and reads the simulator directly, and because a register is not the point either - a curtailment that lands in the right register and changes nothing is a confirmed command that did nothing - every write is checked three ways: the app's status, the raw word on the wire, and what the plant does next, read back through the product's own telemetry. It found one, in the configuration path rather than the write path: a SunSpec profile that declares NO telemetry keys was polled happily and stored nothing at all, in complete silence. A SunSpec profile needs no register bindings because it discovers its datapoints, but a sample whose key the profile does not declare is dropped by design - so a profile with no keys reads the device perfectly and keeps none of it. The poller logged that it was polling every two seconds, the device's Last seen kept advancing (liveness is deliberately recorded before the keys are consulted), no error was raised anywhere, and the only trace was `decoded: 0` buried in GET /api/devices/stats. An integrator sees a connected inverter with an empty chart and nothing to explain it. The register-map mode has always refused the equivalent profile out loud; the SunSpec mode now refuses it too, naming what to fix, with a unit test guarding that no shipped SunSpec builtin ever trips the new refusal. Everything else about the write path held up and is now proven rather than assumed: a curtailment reaches the real holding register, reports confirmed, and the inverter really throttles (its operating state changes to THROTTLED and its AC power drops below the cap); the sign convention is right - a setpoint of -40 on an i16 register is 65496 on the wire, not clamped and not refused, and a 0.1-scaled -12.5 is 65411; every actuation gate holds on the Modbus transport and a refused command leaves the register untouched, which only a read of the wire can show; a write that cannot land (an unmapped register, an unreachable device) ends failed, says plainly it was not confirmed, and is recorded exactly once with nothing retrying it; and the same guarded path works for a NON-SunSpec device through a hand-authored register map, where the vendor inverter throttles to the written limit and recovers when it is lifted. 30/30 against the fix, 29/30 against unfixed main.
- **myiotsan**: Live bench of what happens to a command that does NOT succeed (tools/fleetbench/bench_iotsan_commands.py). myiotsan argues hard for one design decision - an actuation is never automatically retried, because re-sending a relay write is a second physical action and a retry whose first attempt landed opens the door twice - and that promise has two halves. Nothing retries is the easy half. Nothing is silently lost is the half that makes the first one safe: refusing to retry is only defensible because a HUMAN is handed the decision, so a command that reaches no human is a silent drop rather than a safe refusal, and a command left neither confirmed nor failed is worse than a retry because nobody is told, nothing counts it, and the row claims to be in flight forever. The bench found two ways a command was lost, and fixed both. ONE REPORT CONFIRMED THREE COMMANDS. Confirmation matched a device's report against its outstanding commands by comparing the reported NUMBER alone, ignoring which telemetry key the report arrived on. A building controller routinely has several commands outstanding that share a number - a lock and a fan are both switches, so both are 1 - so reporting the lock's state marked the fan `confirmed`, which is the strongest claim this system makes about a physical action, and the fan's command then never became the failure the operator would have been shown. One report invented an actuation and lost a failure in the same stroke. It also confirmed a command whose profile declares NO confirm key, the case the code itself documents as an honest "sent, never confirmed" (a bulb that reports colour per-channel cannot be confirmed against one packed float). Confirmation now requires the report to be on the key the profile declares as that command's ConfirmKey, a command declaring none is never confirmed by telemetry at all, and a device whose profile cannot be read confirms nothing - a timeout and an honest "not confirmed" costs less than a false claim that a relay moved. The matching rule is a pure function (confirmsKey) pinned by a unit test. A COMMAND INTERRUPTED MID-WRITE STAYED PENDING FOREVER. The row is written down BEFORE the send, deliberately and correctly - an actuation that was sent but never recorded is the worst possible ordering - but SweepUnconfirmed only ever looked at rows in `sent`, so a process that stopped in between left a row no timeout applied to: never swept, never counted, never notified, and rendered by the UI as still in flight, a spinner that never resolves. The sweep now ends stale `pending` rows too, timed from RequestedAt because such a row has no SentAt (that being exactly what it never got), with a reason that distinguishes the two cases honestly: an unconfirmed command was definitely sent, an interrupted one may never have left the building. The cutoff is the existing 30s confirm window, comfortably longer than any legitimate stay in pending (the MQTT publish is in-process and the Modbus write is bounded by modbusWriteTimeout). Everything else about the lifecycle held, and is now proven rather than assumed: an unconfirmed command becomes failed on the timeout and NOTHING is re-sent across the whole window; the failure reaches the operator's notification feed; a late report does not resurrect a failed command; the twin still shows the disagreement; a restart with a command in flight ends that command and re-sends nothing; and a wedged Modbus device is dialled exactly once per attempt with no re-dial after a restart. The bench drives the hard cases for real: a socket that accepts and never answers stalls a Modbus write inside its per-operation timeout (the window a docker kill has to land in) and counts dials, which is how "nothing re-drove it" is measured on the polled transport; the device that CONFIRMS is the same real MQTT client that watches the wire, so a confirmation is the product's own loop closing rather than an API poke. 34/34 against the fixes; the identical bench file scores 27/32 against unfixed main (two of the 34 checks - the interruption's reason text and its notification - are only reachable once an interrupted command actually ends).
- **infra**: First live bench of mypintusan, the app that decides who physically walks through a door. It had the thinnest unit coverage in the suite (7 test files) and zero live exercise, and its decision path is a PURE FUNCTION with genuinely good table tests - the shape that lulls, because Decide() can be flawless while the snapshot handed to it is wrong and no test of a pure function will ever notice. tools/fleetbench/pintusan_harness.py stands the app up with a REAL OSDP bus over TCP and tools/osdp-sim on it; tools/fleetbench/bench_pintusan_door.py provisions a site end to end (door, reader, holder, credential, group, schedule, grant) and then presents cards on the wire, asserting only against what the product itself recorded. THE CORE ACCESS PATH HELD UP: 32/32. A valid badge grants and the log names the holder; an unknown card is denied and its raw value still recorded; a revoked credential stops opening the door within one second and is refused for the right reason rather than a generic one; lockdown denies a credential that would otherwise be granted; a duress PIN grants (the door opens for a coerced holder), is recorded as a plain grant with the duress marker only in operator-side fields, and raises a CRITICAL alarm; and a remote unlock is recorded with the operator's name. Two real defects were found and fixed, both small. First, a badge on a reader that ANSWERS PERFECTLY WELL and that nobody has enrolled was filed as reason 'reader-offline' - the one thing it is not. The bench watched a healthy simulated reader deliver cards while the operator was told the reader was offline, which sends whoever is commissioning a new door to check cabling and power for a device whose only problem is that it is not in the database. It now has its own reason code, reader-not-enrolled, mapped and translated in all four languages. Second, in the tooling: tools/osdp-sim's DEFAULT card (deadbeef) fails Wiegand-26 leading even parity, and a CP treats a parity failure as a hard denial because a card one bit out may be somebody else's - so the simulator's own default could never open a door, and the first run of this bench spent itself watching every badge denied. The default is now 00880040 (facility 1, card 4096, valid parity), and the simulator gained a -pin flag so duress is benchable at all: it emits keypad digits BEFORE each card, which is the order the controller requires since it buffers a keypad entry and consumes it when the card arrives.
- **myiotsan,infra**: Live bench of the myiotsan Flow Engine's JavaScript sandbox (tools/fleetbench/bench_iotsan_flows.py) against a real broker, driving what a flow's arbitrary-JavaScript nodes can REACH and what they can COST. The sandbox containment itself held completely - no filesystem/network/require escape, no cross-flow scratchpad leakage, a throwing node only drops its own branch - but the bench found real defects on the cost side. A function/expression/switch node's script used to be validated only at runtime compile, so a typo saved, enabled, listed as 'On' on the flows list, and simply never ran, with a single INFO log line as the only trace; scripts are now compiled at SAVE (a shared nodeScript/checkScripts helper the runtime also compiles, so save and runtime can never disagree) and refused with a 400. flowScriptTimeout (100ms) fenced one script call but nothing fenced an EVENT, so a graph of individually well-behaved scripts could legally spend up to 200 nodes x 100ms = 20s of the ONE shared flow worker on a single reading - measured live at 15.1s for a 190-node graph, stalling every other flow in the install; a new flowEventBudget (1s) now bounds one reading's total cost across the whole graph, and a flow whose script TIMES OUT (not merely throws - an ordinary thrown error is free and does not count) 5 times in a row is quarantined and named in the notification feed until it is edited and re-saved. A stale goja watchdog interrupt could also kill the NEXT, unrelated script if a call finished in the same instant its own watchdog fired; the interrupt is now cleared on the way in as well as the way out. Flow rows now carry a runtimeState (running/error/quarantined/disabled/starting) so 'enabled' and 'actually running' are no longer indistinguishable on screen, and a new GET /api/flows/stats endpoint plus three Prometheus gauges (myiotsan_flow_events_dropped_total, myiotsan_flow_queue_depth, myiotsan_flows_stopped) expose the runtime's dropped-event counter, which used to be written to a field nothing ever read (and was a data race, now atomic). Chasing why an install's 110 enabled flows compiled only 100 traced back one layer down to infra/db/sql, shared by every app: all three drivers (sqlite, postgres, mariadb) hardcoded a 100-row scan ceiling in selectWithSQL regardless of the caller's limit, while the reported total row count still reflected the true, larger result - so a truncated page was indistinguishable from a complete one. Roughly seventy call sites across the suite ask for more than 100 rows; the scan now takes exactly the limit the caller asked for, falling back to the historical 100-row ceiling only when no limit was given at all. 41/41 with all fixes; 32/43 against unfixed main on the identical bench file.
- **myiotsan**: Live bench of myiotsan's deadband gate and telemetry read-back path against a real broker (tools/fleetbench/bench_iotsan_telemetry.py). The deadband gate itself held completely - first-sample admission, the >= boundary, a baseline that does not creep across a slow sub-deadband drift, heartbeat behavior, an edited deadband taking effect with no restart, and a suppressed sample still reaching the rule engine were all measured correct. The bench found real defects on the read-back half. TelemetryService.Latest folded a device's newest 500 rows to build "the current value of every key" for the device page, which crowds a quiet key off the page once a chatty key fills the shared tail (measured: 520 rows on one key hid the other six keys on a seven-key device); it now takes the profile's declared key list and does one indexed seek per key, falling back to the tail scan only for a device with no profile. Series() asked the database ascending, which discards the newest end of a window over the cap - a wide chart of a busy key drew the oldest slice and looked identical to a dead device; it now reads newest-first and reverses, and over the cap actually reads the rollup buckets (Rollups() had zero callers anywhere in the app before this) topped up with the unfolded raw tail, answering {items, span, truncated} instead of a bare list so a chart never silently swaps raw samples for hourly summaries without saying so. rollupOnce's cutoff was now-minus-width rather than a bucket boundary, which cuts mid-bucket and permanently undercounts the readings past the cut; the cutoff is now floored to the bucket boundary, and a new dropIncompleteTail guards a batch-filling pass from folding an unproven-whole trailing bucket. The rollup worker's interval was a hardcoded, unsettable hour, so it had never been observed running on any bench of this app; a new telemetry_store.rollupIntervalMs config field (default unchanged at 1h) makes it exercisable. DeadbandGate.Forget shipped with zero production callers despite the ingest path documenting the gate map as its one unbounded structure; device deletion now calls a new Ingest.ForgetDevice. ReadingWriter's written stat summed CreateMultiple's last-insert-id return value instead of counting rows, so it grew with table size rather than work done (2,666 readings reported as 3,555,111 written); it now counts rows handed to each batch. 50/50 checks passed with the fixes, 33/44 against the unfixed binary.
- **myiotsan**: myiotsan had zero screen checks - every bench it has ever had was an API bench - so the first one (tools/fleetbench/uicheck_iotsan.js, driving all eleven nav sections in headless Chrome across en/ms/zh/ar and all three roles, plus workflow and permission-parity parts B and C) found three real defects invisible from the API side. First and worst: every non-admin was completely locked out of the UI. GET /api/auth/session - the route the SPA asks on boot to learn who it is talking to - was absent from services/rbac.go's deny-by-default permission catalog, so an operator or viewer who signed in successfully was answered 403 on that probe, App.js treated any non-OK answer as 'nobody is signed in', and the sign-in card came back forever with no error at any layer; every prior bench of this app ran as admin, which bypasses the matrix entirely, so nothing had ever exercised the path. Fixed by adding /api/auth/session to the catalog as read for viewer and operator. Second: a refusal was rendered as an absence, twice - the SPA now tells 403 apart from 401 on the session probe (a new NoAccessScreen replaces the login-card loop), and a viewer legitimately refused telemetry history no longer sees nine empty charts saying 'No readings in this period' (indistinguishable from a dead sensor); loadSeries now carries denied:r.status===403 and the charts say plainly that the account cannot see reading history. Third: the Add-device form offered a 'http' protocol option this app has never had a route to receive, so a device created that way was provisioned, enabled and permanently mute with no way to correct it; the form now offers mqtt only and services/device.go gained a closed supportedProtocols set (mqtt, modbus) that Create refuses anything else against. 114/114 checks pass with the fixes; 93/106 against the unfixed app (locked-out roles attempt fewer checks because device-stage checks are gated on opening a device). Also confirmed good and unchanged: all eleven sections render correctly in all four languages including RTL Arabic, actuation confirmation/cancel/audit-row behavior, and create/delete round-tripping through the screen.
- **infra**: First live bench of mypintusan's OSDP Secure Channel (tools/fleetbench/bench_pintusan_securechannel.py, 18 checks), item 1 of a new hardening register for the app that physically opens doors, which had exactly one live bench before this. Secure Channel is the only thing standing between a screwdriver and a building on an RS-485 bus, and its two security-critical fault-table rows - a reader that refuses the handshake, and a session that drops mid-conversation - had never actually been driven against a live appliance: tools/osdp-sim's refuse-sc/no-sc/wrong-key scenarios presented no card at all, so they could show a session being refused but not what a badge does next, which is the half of 'must fail closed' that matters. The simulator now runs a card loop on all three so that claim can actually be demonstrated. Each bench episode is a fresh boot, because a reader's key and its RequireSecureChannel policy are seeded from config on first boot only and there is no API to change either afterwards. TWO REAL DEFECTS FOUND AND FIXED. (1) A session that established and then DROPPED mid-conversation left the door granting on cleartext: infra/access/osdp/bus.go's secureChannelLost only re-announced the downgrade from StatusSecuring (a handshake that never came up), so a reader already StatusOnline when its session died produced no event and every consumer kept the SecureSession:true it was told at handshake, permanently. Measured against the simulator's sc-drop scenario, a door requiring an encrypted session went on granting badges on a reader whose session had died - precisely the RS-485 tap this mechanism exists to defeat. Fixed by re-announcing from StatusOnline too. (2) A critical or perimeter door created without mentioning Secure Channel did not require one: entities/door.go's own field comment has always said RequireSecureChannel 'defaults on for perimeter and critical', but the create handler passed the request body's boolean straight through while every neighbouring field (UnlockSeconds, HeldOpenSeconds, OfflinePolicy) got a real default. Measured live, a card on a plaintext reader opened a critical door. Fixed with a new entities.SecureChannelDefault(class) function and turning the create request's field into a *bool so an explicit false is distinguishable from silence; this mattered more than an ordinary defaulting bug because there is no PUT /api/doors, so a door created with the wrong policy kept it for good. Two new unit tests cover the default in apps/mypintusan/services/decision_test.go. PROVEN GOOD AND UNCHANGED: a healthy secure session grants; no-sc and refuse-sc both deny with the specific reason secure-channel-failed rather than a vaguer one; a door that does not require SC still works with a reader that cannot do it; a key mismatch never becomes a cleartext session and is alarmed. A FEATURE GAP WAS FOUND AND REPORTED, NOT BUILT: Reader.ScbkState is written once at creation as 'default' and never updated - ScbkRekeyed/ScbkFailed are declared in entities/reader.go and assigned nowhere, and although infra/access/osdp already answers a PDCAP capability request and carries the result as Event.DefaultKey/DefaultKeySession, nothing in apps/mypintusan reads either field, so a reader still on the factory default key is never capped to interior-only as the design intends. 18/18 checks pass with the fixes; 13/18 against the unfixed app.
- **infra**: Asked, for the first time, whether mypintusan's six declared alarms can actually fire (tools/fleetbench/bench_pintusan_alarms.py, 19 checks; item 2 of the hardening register for the app that physically opens doors). services/controller.go declares duress, tamper, reader-offline, secure-channel, door-forced and door-held-open, each with a severity, an operator headline written to be read at 3am and a translation in four languages - and THREE OF THE SIX COULD NOT FIRE ON ANY INSTALLATION. One omission on the wire explains all three: an OSDP PD answers POLL with an ACK, or with a queued card read or keypad entry, and NEVER volunteers a status change, so LSTAT (tamper, mains) and ISTAT (the door-position contact) have to be scheduled by the CP - and infra/access/osdp's poll loop sent neither, ever. Everything downstream of that hole was built and unit-tested: the door state machine, Controller.ContactChanged, the forced and held-open events, the audit reasons, the CRITICAL severity on door-forced, and a TestBusTamperSurfaces that passed for the life of the driver by handing the bus an LSTAT itself. Measured live against a real appliance and tools/osdp-sim: opening a reader's enclosure raised nothing, a door opening with no grant and no exit request raised nothing, and a door wedged open past its threshold raised nothing - the last being the commonest way a secure door stops being one. A FOURTH DEFECT sat in the other half of an alarm: Reader.TamperState was written once as 'ok' when the reader was enrolled and never again, and Reader.LastSeenAt was never written at all, so while the reader-offline alarm fired correctly the readers screen still reported tamperState 'ok', lastSeenAt 0 - the alarm woke somebody and the screen they opened lied to them. FIXED by giving the poll loop a supervision schedule (osdp.Options.StatusInterval, default 1s, statusMillis per bus in config and runtime settings) that alternates LSTAT and ISTAT, skips ISTAT entirely on a reader whose PDCAP claims no supervised inputs (asking a keypad-only reader for a door contact earns a NAK every second, and a NAK is a failed transaction, which is what declares a healthy reader offline), and emits both on the EDGE so a reader whose case is left open reports once rather than once a second forever; by a new osdp.EventInput carrying the ISTATR contact states, which Controller.contactReported maps to the door bound to that reader and feeds to ContactChanged (input 0 is the door contact and true means open - a convention the driver now documents, since OSDP reports an input's electrical state and leaves the meaning to the installer); and by Controller.markReader + SQLStore.MarkReader persisting supervision state onto the reader row, with LastSeenAt deliberately NOT stamped when a reader is declared offline because it was last seen when it last answered, not when we gave up on it. A DEFECT THIS WORK INTRODUCED AND THE EXISTING SUITE CAUGHT, recorded because the reason is a real production failure: gating supervision only on elapsed time starved card delivery, because a card is queued at the PD and handed over on a POLL and every dead reader on a segment costs a full reply timeout - so a degraded bus can take longer to go round than the status interval, at which point every single transaction is a status command and NO BADGE IS EVER DELIVERED while the bus looks up and supervision looks healthy. Supervision is now capped at one slot in four per reader (minPollsPerStatus) as well as by time, pinned by TestBusStatusNeverStarvesCardDelivery. DoorMachine.ContactBound now answers from both sources - a myiotsan contact is CONFIGURED, a contact on the reader's supervised input is DISCOVERED - because answering only from configuration told an operator they had no forced-door detection on every door whose contact is wired the ordinary way. The simulator needed two new scenarios (contact-open, contact-cycle: the PD had always modelled Inputs and nothing had ever driven them), and tamper/silent gained card loops for the same reason refuse-sc/no-sc/wrong-key did in the previous item - a reader that never came up produces no grants and no alarms, which reads exactly like a reader that came up and whose alarm is dead. The bench's most important episode is the negative one, that a granted entry must NOT raise a forced alarm, and it is only meaningful because the door's held-open threshold is set below how long the contact stays open: door-held-open firing is the positive evidence that the opening reached the state machine, and only then is the forced alarm's absence a statement about the shunt. Seven new tests (TestBusPollsStatusUnasked, TestBusReportsDoorContactUnasked, TestBusStatusIsEdgeTriggered, TestBusStatusNeverStarvesCardDelivery, TestReaderContactRaisesForcedAlarm, TestReaderContactAfterGrantDoesNotAlarm, TestReaderTamperMarksTheReaderRow). PROVEN GOOD AND UNCHANGED: duress fires at CRITICAL (re-run as the control for the whole notification path, so that 'no alarm arrived' elsewhere is never confusable with 'the feed is broken'), and reader-offline fires at WARNING rather than CRITICAL, which is the deliberate choice alarm.go argues for. STILL NOT BUILT, REPORTED RATHER THAN PAPERED OVER: the myiotsan door-contact binding (Door.ContactDeviceKey) has no path in, so a site whose contacts terminate on a relay board rather than on the reader still has no forced or held-open detection; and Controller.RequestToExit has no caller, so a REX button wired to the reader does not shunt the forced alarm. 19/19 checks pass with the fixes; 9/19 against the unfixed app (the simulator changes are bench tooling and were held constant across both runs).
- **infra**: Asked, for the first time, whether mypintusan's offline mode and its cache TTL can actually be observed on a running appliance (tools/fleetbench/bench_pintusan_offline.py, 19 checks over 8 episodes; item 3 of the hardening register for the app that physically opens doors, after Secure Channel and the six alarms). Decide()'s GATE 10 is the app's stated defence against the oldest attack on an access-control system - past the TTL the door denies, and there is deliberately no allow-all option because 'fail open on network loss' is a documented attack (cut the uplink, walk in) - and its unit tests are good, which is exactly the shape that lulls, since the gate can be flawless while nothing on a real controller ever feeds it a number. FOUR DEFECTS, all of the same shape as the six-alarms bench before it: the gate was right and nothing could reach it. (1) THE CACHE COULD NEVER EXPIRE. ControllerConfig.CacheAge was declared and compared against each door's TTL in Decide()'s GATE 10, and nothing assigned it anywhere outside a unit test; in production it was nil, so Snapshot.CacheAge was always zero, so s.CacheAge > ttl was always false, so the reason code offline-cache-expired could not be produced on any install ever. Measured live: a door 20 seconds past a 2-second TTL still granted. Fixed by new apps/mypintusan/services/cache_clock.go (services.CacheClock) - measures the time since anything entitled to change the access rules last reached this controller, persisted in a runtime_setting row (access.lastContact) so a reboot cannot reset staleness to zero, writes throttled to once a minute. Two contact signals reset it: the fleet control channel being live (a new nil-safe SetOnContact hook on domain/shared/fleetnode's control channel, fired on hello and on every parent frame including the keepalive pong), and any accepted administrative change to the access rules on the appliance itself (a new ruleChangeTouch middleware in apps/mypintusan/app/app.go, scoped by path prefix and to 2xx responses only). (2) offlinePolicy: deny AND offlineTtlSeconds WERE UNREACHABLE. createDoorRequest had neither field; the create handler hardcoded OfflinePolicy: entities.OfflineCached and left the TTL at 0 (the class default of 8/24/72 hours). There is no PUT /api/doors, so every door on every install was born cached with a class-default TTL and kept it for good. Both fields are now accepted on create; offlinePolicy is validated to cached|deny and anything else - notably any spelling of allow - is REFUSED with an explicit message rather than silently coerced, because there is no fail-open policy in this product; a negative TTL is refused too. (3) TURNING OFFLINE MODE ON DID NOT REACH THE RUNNING CONTROLLERS. The runtime read Offline once at process start. A PUT /api/settings/access with offline: true persisted, returned 200 and read back true while every door carried on deciding as though online. ControllerConfig.Offline became func() bool (a live question, not a boot-time fact) backed by an atomic on the runtime; IAccessSettingsService gained OnChange, published on Save and Reset; runtime.ApplySettings applies offline live. Only offline applies live - buses, reader keys, timers and the site timezone still need a restart, and the Settings screen's restart note was reworded to name exactly which fields that covers. (4) NOBODY WAS TOLD THE SITE WAS DEGRADED. docs/MYPINTUSAN_DATA_MODEL.md section 2 has said since P1 that a controller serving from cache raises a degraded-mode alert immediately; nothing raised one, and a cached grant is byte-identical to a live one at the door and on every screen. New alarm kind services.AlarmDegraded (degraded), WARNING severity, headline Running from cache - access rules cannot be updated, raised on the transition into offline mode AND at boot for an appliance that starts offline (which never crosses the edge and would otherwise run degraded forever with nobody told). ALSO ADDED: offline mode had no control on the Settings screen at all, so the only way to enable it was editing config.json before first boot; a checkbox was added (settings.offlineHead/offline/offlineHint, translated in en/ms/zh/ar). THE POSITIVE CONTROL IS LOAD-BEARING: episode 3 badges a critical door with a holder not flagged OfflineAllowed and requires the offline-not-allowed denial, which comes from nowhere else in the app - without it, 'the TTL did not deny' and 'the gate never ran at all' would be the same observation. PROVEN GOOD and recorded as such: GATE 10 does run on a live controller; Holder.OfflineAllowed was already reachable via POST /api/holders and works end to end; the access log's offline flag is set correctly; a revoked credential is denied credential-revoked and not masked by a stale cache (reported UNPROVABLE rather than a false pass on a run where the cache never actually expires); and no allow-all offline policy can be created, before or after the fix. TWO THINGS DELIBERATELY NOT BUILT, recorded as open gaps rather than papered over: there is still no PUT /api/doors and no doors-admin screen (doors are only created by the first-run wizard), so the new offline policy fields are reachable through the API only; and there is no replica-refresh mechanism anywhere in the suite - myseliasan pushes no access data down to a node, and no other route does either - so the appliance's own database remains its only authority and offline mode stays an operator-declared deployment mode rather than something the controller detects for itself. docs/MYPINTUSAN_DATA_MODEL.md section 2's old sentence describing the cache as 'a full local replica...refreshed on change' described a topology that never existed and has been corrected to say so. 19/19 checks pass with the fixes; 12/19 against the unfixed app.
- **infra**: Asked, for the first time, whether mypintusan's schedules, holiday calendar and site timezone survive contact with a running appliance (tools/fleetbench/bench_pintusan_schedules.py, 29 checks over 8 episodes; item 4 of the hardening register for the app that physically opens doors, after Secure Channel, the six alarms and offline mode). Decide()'s GATE 8 is the only gate whose answer changes with nobody touching the system, and every part of it is site-local: Snapshot.Location exists because a night shift that runs 22:00-06:00 and a public holiday are both wrong if evaluated in UTC. Its unit tests are good and all of them BUILD the snapshot they check, which is exactly the shape that lulls - scheduleAllows can be flawless while the window an operator types, the zone the site runs in, or the site a holiday is scoped to never reaches it. HOW THE CLOCK IS DRIVEN: a bench cannot wait until 02:00 to ask whether 02:00 is inside a night-shift window, and it does not have to - IANA zones span 26 hours of offset, so for any instant there is a real fixed-offset zone in which the wall clock is already at any hour you want, and each episode boots the appliance into one. That is not a simulation of the night shift, it is the night shift, somewhere. FOUR DEFECTS. (1) THE ONE WAY GATE 8 COULD FAIL OPEN. A window whose end equals its start fell into the wrapping (overnight) branch, where 'after the start' is true from the first minute of the day and the previous day's tail catches the rest - so a 09:00-09:00 window matched every hour of every day, on all seven weekdays, while the schedules screen labelled it '(overnight)'. Measured live: such a schedule opened a door at 02:51 that was meant to be shut until 09:00. The way in is not a typo on a form: Go's decoder drops unknown fields silently, so a client sending field names the API does not read has every window arrive as 0-0 while the 'a schedule needs at least one window' guard happily passes - which is precisely what four of this app's own bench scripts did (they posted startMinute/endMinute; the API reads startMin/endMin), so #211, #219, #220 and #221 all ran their whole lives on an accidental 24/7 schedule they believed was office hours. POST /api/schedules now refuses a zero-length window with a message naming the 24/7 flag, refuses a window starting at 24:00, and windowCovers treats a stored zero-length window as covering NOTHING so rows already written on an install cannot open a door; the four bench scripts now say always: true, which is what they meant. (2) CORRECTING THE SITE TIMEZONE DID NOT REACH THE RUNNING CONTROLLER. It is the FIRST thing the setup wizard asks for ('tell us where this site is, so opening hours and holidays are right'), on a controller that is already running, and it was read once at boot - so every fresh install evaluated its schedules and its holiday calendar in whatever zone config.json seeded, which on an appliance is UTC, until somebody power-cycled it. Measured live: with the site corrected to a zone where it was 12:22, a 09:00-17:00 grant went on being refused out-of-schedule because the controller was still deciding at 03:22. ControllerConfig.Location became func() *time.Location, asked freshly on every badge and backed by an atomic pointer on the runtime; runtime.ApplySettings swaps it on a settings save and REFUSES a zone that will not load rather than silently falling back to UTC. The bus list, the reader keys and the tick remain boot-only - re-reading those means tearing down every segment on the site - and the Settings screen's restart note (en/ms/zh/ar) was reworded to name only those. (3) A HOLIDAY COULD NOT BE SCOPED TO A SITE, BECAUSE NO DOOR COULD BE AT ONE. HolidayOn prefers a row matching the door's SiteId over a global one - the entire reason Holiday is its own entity, since Malaysian public holidays vary BY STATE - and store_sql_test.go tests that precedence with SiteId 5. No request shape ever carried a site onto a door, so every door on every install was at site 0, the site branch could never match, and a site-scoped holiday closed nothing anywhere. siteId is now accepted on POST /api/doors (0 still means 'follow the global calendar', right for the single-site appliance this mostly ships as); same shape as the offline policy fields before it, and it matters for the same reason - there is no PUT /api/doors, so a door keeps whatever placement it was born with. (4) NOTHING RECORDED WHO CHANGED THE CALENDAR. Creating or deleting a holiday is the one rule change that closes - or REOPENS - every door on a site for a whole day without anybody editing a grant, and it was the only access-rule mutation that published no access.rule-change notification; creating a schedule was missing one too. Both now publish, with the schedule's hours rendered into the audit line so a mistake is visible in it. ALSO ADDED: the holiday calendar had an API, a decision gate, a denial reason translated into four languages and client functions in lib/access.js - and NO SCREEN. Nothing in the app ever called listHolidays, createHoliday or deleteHoliday, so the calendar was empty on every install and the branch of scheduleAllows that reads it could not run. Access.js gained a Holidays section (admin-only, deny by default because that is what a public holiday means and it is the fail-closed answer, date entered as a calendar day in the site's zone, removal confirmed because it reopens a building), translated in en/ms/zh/ar. PROVEN GOOD and recorded as such, so it is not re-benched: the night shift works from BOTH sides (22:00-06:00 grants at 23:21 on its own weekday and still grants at 03:22 the next morning, and the wrapped tail correctly does NOT cover the day the window opened); the same rule at the same instant grants at a site where it is 12:22 and denies where it is 03:22, so the timezone genuinely decides; all three holiday behaviours work (deny closes and says holiday, ignore leaves the day ordinary, follow-sunday runs the Sunday timetable - checked in both directions, with a schedule that has no Sunday window and with one that has only a Sunday window); deleting a holiday reopens the door; and a holiday is resolved on the SITE's calendar date, not the server's, verified at a site whose local date was a day ahead of UTC. 29/29 checks pass with the fixes; 22/29 against the unfixed app.
- **infra**: Asked, for the first time, whether the WIRE under mypintusan's decisions survives what actually happens to an RS-485 segment in a building (tools/fleetbench/bench_pintusan_bus.py, 42 checks over 10 episodes; item 5 of the hardening register for the app that physically opens doors, after Secure Channel, the six alarms, offline mode and schedules). It drives the rest of the fault table in docs/MYPINTUSAN_OSDP_PLAN.md 4.1 - busy, bad-sequence, bad-crc, garbage, one-down, slow, addr-collision - and it is the first exercise of the reconnect path at all. WHY IT TAKES A LIVE APPLIANCE: bus_test.go is 733 lines and good, and every one of its cases runs over an in-memory net.Pipe that is never torn down mid-run, so the chain from failPort through Run returning, superviseBus re-dialling, a fresh Bus and Controller being built and the site's lockdown being carried into them has no coverage at that layer and cannot have one. Killing a simulator is the only way to ask, and that takes a real process to kill. FOUR DEFECTS. (1) THE FAULT WITH THE BIGGEST BLAST RADIUS WAS THE ONLY SILENT ONE. A reader that stops answering is alarmed, because Run keeps polling and each timeout counts against OfflineAfter. A dead PORT is not: failPort ends the run on the very next slot - deliberately, so the owner can re-dial - which is long before any reader has failed the three transactions that declare it offline. So unplugging the adapter took every door on the segment out of service, raised NO alarm of any kind, and left every reader row reading 'ok, last seen a moment ago'. Measured live: forty-five seconds after the cable came out, the alarm feed held NOTHING AT ALL - not one notification of any category. New services.Controller.BusDown marks every reader on the segment offline - without moving LastSeenAt, which belongs to when the reader last ANSWERED, not to when we gave up on it - and raises one new AlarmBusOffline ('OSDP segment down - every door on it is out of service') naming the port and the reader count. One alarm for the cable rather than one per reader, because eight pages for one cable is how people learn to dismiss alarms - and one alarm per OUTAGE rather than per failed re-dial, since a gateway that is rebooting accepts the connection and drops it again for as long as that takes, and paging on each of those would deliver the alarm-fatigue failure alarm.go argues against through the very alarm added to prevent silence; a session lasting 20s ends the outage and re-arms it, the same threshold that resets the backoff in (3). Both halves matter: marking the rows is what stops the screen lying to the person the alarm just woke. (2) EVERY PROTOCOL FAULT REACHED THE OPERATOR AS 'READER SECURE CHANNEL FAULT'. osdp.EventFault covers a skewed sequence number, a NAKed command, an undecodable PDID, a card read that will not parse AND a failed Secure Channel handshake, and the controller mapped the whole kind to AlarmSecureChannel. Measured against the bad-sequence scenario, on a reader with no Secure Channel configured at all: headline 'Reader secure channel fault', body 'reply sequence 1, expected 0 - resetting the session'. That is the right alarm under a headline that sends an installer hunting for a bus tap while the actual fault is cabling or firmware. Fixed with Event.Fault (FaultProtocol / FaultSecureChannel; the zero value is FaultProtocol so an unclassified fault errs towards 'wiring' rather than 'security', because over-reporting security faults is how an operator learns to ignore them), set at the three secure-channel emission sites in bus.go, and a new AlarmBusFault - 'Reader communication fault - check the wiring'. This was recorded as an unproven lead by the alarms bench and is now settled. (3) THE RE-DIAL BACKOFF NEVER RESET, SO EVERY RECOVERY MADE THE NEXT ONE SLOWER, PERMANENTLY. superviseBus's 1s-to-30s backoff exists so an absent adapter does not spin, and is capped so that 'a door that comes back must not wait minutes to be polled again'. It doubled on every attempt and was never returned to its floor, so it accumulated over the life of the PROCESS rather than over the current outage: a site whose gateway reboots nightly, or whose cable is knocked by a cleaner once a week, reached the cap after about five events and stayed there. Measured live: after four knocks and thirty seconds of perfectly healthy running the sixth reconnect took 28.5s, against 5.2s for the same knock on the fixed build - so every door on the segment stayed dead for half a minute after the cable was already back, which is the exact delay the cap exists to prevent. Fixed: a session that ran for 20s or more resets the backoff to its floor, because a session that RAN is the end of an outage, not a step deeper into one. (4) A SEGMENT THAT CANNOT ENROL A READER LEAKED ITS CONNECTION ON EVERY RE-DIAL. runBus dials first and enrols second, and Bus closes the port in Run's defer and nowhere else - so the 'no usable readers' path returned without closing anything while superviseBus retried for ever. One requireSecureChannel with no key, or one mistyped 16-byte SCBK, on a single-reader segment is enough to take that path, and both are refused per-reader on purpose (a mistyped key must never silently become a cleartext session). Measured: 10 established connections after 75s of re-dialling against a simulator with no reader being polled, where the fixed build holds none - and bounded only by Go's finalizer eventually closing sockets the process no longer references, which is a garbage-collection schedule and not a resource policy. It matters more than an ordinary leak because the far end is usually a serial-to-Ethernet gateway and those commonly accept one to four TCP clients, so correcting the typo does not necessarily recover the segment: the gateway's client table is full of connections nobody is using and somebody has to go and power-cycle it. Fixed with a defer that closes the transport on every path that does not reach Run. PROVEN GOOD and recorded as such, so it is not re-benched: a sick reader does not take out a healthy one (three readers on one cable, the middle one dies, it is alarmed and its row goes offline while doors 1 and 3 both still open within seconds with their rows still reading ok); nor does a slow one (a reader answering at 800ms against a 1000ms timeout is not declared offline, and the healthy reader beside it grants on a cadence the cable sustains); BUSY is retried rather than treated as death; a reader that is present but unusable does not open a door and is declared offline by the supervision timeout; the three-way awaitReply diagnosis reaches the operator VERBATIM and is the most valuable thing in this area (bad-crc produces 'frame(s) arrived but failed CRC - check 120 ohm termination at both ends, shield grounded at one end only, and cable length', addr-collision produces 'bytes received but no frame decoded - likely two readers sharing address 0, or a badly terminated segment'); the appliance keeps serving through corrupted CRCs, junk with stray SOMs and two PDs fighting over address 0; and LOCKDOWN SURVIVES AN UNPLUGGED ADAPTER END TO END - sealed, badge denied lockdown, cable pulled, the API still reporting the site sealed while the segment is down, cable back, a badge on the RECONNECTED bus still denied lockdown, then lifted and the same badge granted.
