REPORTED, NOT BUILT: Bus.Stats() returns a full PDStats per reader (transactions, timeouts, CRC errors, NAKs, BUSY replies, sequence errors, offline transitions, secure-channel failures, unframed bytes) and its own comment says why they matter - 'a bus with failing termination or a ground loop shows a rising CrcErrors long before any reader actually drops offline' - and nothing outside a test calls it; nor does anything call Bus.Dropped(), the count of events discarded because the consumer stalled, which is to say badges that never reached a decision. No API field, no endpoint, no screen. TOOLING: five more simulator scenarios could not demonstrate their own claim because they presented no card at all - busy, bad-sequence, bad-crc, garbage and addr-collision are each a claim about a DOOR, and none of them could offer a credential. All five now run a cardLoop, which is what lets this bench say 'no door opens on a cable whose frames cannot be trusted' rather than 'no frame was decoded'; addr-collision badges at address 0 from BOTH colliding PDs, which is the out-of-box case as an installer meets it. pintusan_harness.boot() gained readers= (a multi-PD segment - one-down, multidrop and slow are unaskable on a one-reader bus) plus slot_millis / reply_timeout_millis / status_millis. 42/42 checks pass with the fixes; 36/42 against the unfixed app.
- **infra**: Drove mypintusan's SCREENS for the first time (tools/fleetbench/bench_pintusan_screens.py + uicheck_pintusan.js/_partb/_partc, 166 checks over six headless-Chrome passes: every section in all four languages including RTL Arabic, admin workflows confirmed against the server, and the operator and viewer roles compared control-by-control against what the matrix would actually allow). Item 6 of the hardening register for the app that physically opens doors, and the last app in the suite with zero screen coverage after six API benches. FIVE DEFECTS, four of them making the non-admin half of the product unusable, plus one nobody could have found without looking at a picture. (1) THE OPERATOR ROLE'S ONE POWER DID NOT EXIST. services/rbac.go argues at length that opening a door remotely is an operator power - 'what a receptionist does all day' - and wrote the rule as '/api/doors/unlock'. The route is /api/doors/{id}/unlock. The matcher is segment-wise, so a three-segment rule can never match a four-segment request; the most specific rule that DID match was '/api/doors', which is read-only, so every remote open by an operator was refused on every install. (2) THE SESSION PROBE WAS NOT IN THE CATALOG, so it was denied by default: a viewer or operator signed in successfully - /api/auth/login is public and answered 200 - and GET /api/auth/session was then refused, `user` stayed null, and the SPA rendered the sign-in card again. Correct credentials, no error, straight back to the login screen, forever, while every endpoint the catalog grants them answered 200 to a direct request. (3) THE HOME SCREEN WAS AN ERROR PAGE FOR EVERY NON-ADMIN. Doors.js loaded the door list and the lockdown state in one Promise.all, and lockdown was admin-only, so a refused read did not hide a pill - it rejected the whole load and rendered 'you do not have permission for this action' where the doors should be. The two loads now settle independently, and SEEING that the site is sealed is now granted separately from sealing it: an operator who cannot see a lockdown cannot explain why nothing is opening, which is the exact minute they most need the screen. (4) `write` GRANTS POST AND NOTHING ELSE. '/api/holders' was Operator: write, so an operator could enrol a person and got 403 on the list - the first call the People screen makes. Now readWrite, with the trap named on the constant. (5) THE ROLES WERE UNASSIGNABLE. EnsureRoles seeds viewer, operator and admin on every boot and NOTHING served a route that could put a person in one, so an appliance had exactly one account and every distinction the catalog draws was theoretical: new apis/users.go (roles + user CRUD + password reset, on the shared appliance user service so bcrypt, sessions and the last-administrator guard stay one implementation) and a Users section on the Settings screen. ROOT CAUSE OF THE SCREEN HALF, fixed rather than patched: this app decided authorization TWICE - the server with the deny-by-default matrix, the SPA with a client-side user.isAdmin - and the two drifted in BOTH directions at once (a viewer was offered an Unlock button on every door card and an 'Add person' button; an operator granted read on grants and schedules had the entire Access rules section hidden). New apis/capabilities.go answers GET /api/auth/capabilities by calling Authorize on the REAL route each control would send, and App.js, Doors.js, People.js, Access.js, Readers.js and Settings.js all render from it, so an offer on screen cannot disagree with a decision on the server and the screens follow the catalog when it changes. AND THE ONE THE ASSERTIONS COULD NEVER SEE: opening the screenshots showed every table on every screen rendering RAW WHITE BROWSER BUTTONS labelled 'Sort' jammed under each column heading, in all four languages, on every install - @shared/DataTable ships markup and no style ('styled by the consuming app's stylesheet', its own header says) and mypintusan was the only app in the suite that used it and never wrote them. Ported from rbac-standard.css and rebound to this app's warm palette, with the pager arrows mirrored for Arabic. Every check passed while that was on screen: the screens rendered, every control was hit-testable, no dictionary key leaked, the API answered correctly. ALSO FIXED: the Readers screen computed its Security column from the admin-only access settings and swallowed the refusal, so a non-admin saw 'Not encrypted' against readers running encrypted sessions - a confident wrong answer about the security of a door, on the screen that exists to answer exactly that; it now says 'Not shown' and says why. The Access rules screen renders read-only for an operator, with a line explaining it, rather than offering edits the server refuses. New services/rbac_test.go is the first test this catalog has ever had - it seeds the REAL catalog through the REAL permission service and asserts every rule governs a route the app serves AND every route is covered by a rule, in both directions, which is what would have caught (1) and (2) at build time. domain/shared/services.PathGoverns exports the matcher so any app's catalog can be tested the same way. 166/166 checks pass with the fixes; 91/96 against the unfixed app, where the whole operator and viewer half was unreachable and therefore unmeasurable.
- **mymatasan**: Live view: the saved-positions/home/guard-tours button beside the PTZ ring was an oval, not a circle like the speaker button next to it. It sets a 26x26 box, but the global `button` rule's `min-height: 38px` outranks `block-size`, so it rendered 26x38 - the same trap the audio mute button already carries a comment about. Overridden with `min-block-size: 0`.
- **mymatasan**: The camera AI rules editor could not edit a rule. `withRouting` — the one place every config-rewriting path goes through, so that a mode change cannot drop a rule's destinations, PTZ recall or relay — was written as `const withRouting = (config) => applyPTZRecall(withRouting(config), ruleRecall)`. A function whose body calls itself with the same argument recurses until the stack dies, and SEVEN call sites route through it: changing the detection mode, the target classes, the line geometry, the crowd count, the dwell duration and the LPR config all threw RangeError and left the editor doing nothing. Shipped in #193 (W3-5b). It now re-applies destinations, PTZ recall and relay, which is what its own comment says it is for and what the destinations path already did by hand. ALSO FIXED, in the same screen: a face rule was invisible to the editor. `face` was missing from detectionModes, and modeFromDetectionType falls back to 'presence' for anything it does not know — so a face rule opened as a Presence rule and SAVING IT REWROTE detectionType to 'presence', silently ending face recognition on that camera and leaving a rule that fires on every person and car instead. A mode the editor cannot name is a rule the editor destroys. Face is now a first-class mode with its own panel (match mode, watchlist, minimum match confidence), the class picker is hidden for it exactly as for LPR, and two Save guards that assumed every rule has target classes and a zone are relaxed for it — a face rule has neither, so Save was permanently disabled and the rule could be opened but never saved. The zone is left EMPTY rather than seeded with the default polygon: empty means the whole frame, and quietly shrinking a doorway rule's coverage to the middle 70% of the picture during an unrelated save is worse than having no zone at all. Verified on the wire: the mode list now offers Face recognition, switching an ordinary rule to Crowd and toggling its classes works with an empty console, and a face rule survives an editor round trip (POST 200, detectionType still 'face', zonePolygon still empty, the new matchMode persisted).
- **mymatasan**: Enrolling a passport photo failed with 'no face found in the image - use a clear, front-facing photo'. The photo was fine; the message was blaming it for a property of the DETECTOR'S INPUT. YuNet is anchor-based: it finds faces within a band of sizes RELATIVE to its input frame. A live camera frame - about 640px wide with a small distant face - sits comfortably inside that band, which is why recognition worked on cameras while the upload button refused the very photos people reach for. An uploaded photo is outside the band at BOTH ends. Measured against the shipped model on a plain-background portrait, at native size: 640x480 with the head at 35% of the height finds the face; 413x531 (a 35x45mm passport photo at 300dpi) with the head at 75% finds NOTHING; 1200x1600 at 60% finds nothing; 2448x3264 at 85% finds nothing. Four of five ordinary framings were refused, and every instruction the message gave ('use a clear, front-facing photo') sent the operator to look for a better photo, which cannot help. THE FIX is an enrolment ladder in ai/face_model.py: DOWNSCALE to a longest side of 640 (a 3000px-wide photo puts the face above the anchor range) and PAD with a margin (a head filling the frame leaves no context, and padding makes the face relatively smaller), tried as pad fractions 0.0/0.35/0.75/1.5 each followed by the downscale, stopping at the first rung that finds a face. The padding is a CONSTANT fill of the median edge colour rather than BORDER_REPLICATE: replicating the edge of a photo whose subject touches the border smears a face into the new margin, and a smeared half-face reported as a second face would trade 'no face found' for 'more than one face in the image'. Detection runs on the transformed copy and the rows are mapped BACK to original coordinates - using the per-axis scale actually applied after integer rounding, not the requested one, or every landmark lands slightly off - so alignCrop and the faceprint are computed at full resolution rather than from a 640px thumbnail. THE LADDER IS OFF FOR THE LIVE STAGE on purpose: live frames are already in the comfortable band, and an empty frame (the common case, many times a second) would otherwise cost four detections instead of one. Verified through the real path: faces_worker.py finds one face in a passport photo and in a 2448x3264 file, and both enrol over HTTP with quality 0.86/0.88. New bench tools/fleetbench/bench_face_enrol_framing.py asserts five framings each yield EXACTLY one face (the Go side refuses anything else) AND that each box lands on the subject - a mis-mapped box would still report one face and then embed the wrong pixels, which is a bad enrolment that poisons every future match instead of an error somebody can see. It draws its subject, so no photograph of a real person is committed. ErrNoFace now asks for 'the whole head visible', which after this fix is the one framing still refused and the only advice that helps.
- **mymatasan**: A person STANDING IN FRONT OF A CAMERA was not detected, so face recognition wrote no alert, recorded no clip, sent no notification, and the roster never said 'last seen'. The feature looked switched on and did nothing, which is the worst way for a security feature to fail. SAME ROOT CAUSE AS THE PASSPORT PHOTO, at the other end of it: YuNet finds faces within a band of sizes RELATIVE to its input frame, and the live stage detected at the frame's NATIVE resolution. Measured against the shipped model on a 1920x1080 frame - the resolution a face rule forces - a face is found while it is roughly 7-20% of the frame height (somebody across the room) and NOT FOUND at 30%, 42% or 60% (somebody approaching, standing at the camera, or filling it). Every close-range framing, which is the entire point of a door camera and the first thing anybody tests, was invisible. THE FIX: detect on a 640-long copy first, then at native size, stopping at the first rung that finds a face, with rows mapped back to original coordinates either way so the crop and the faceprint still come from the full-resolution frame. The cheap rung runs first on purpose - measured here, 13ms against 88ms native - so the case that was broken is now the FASTEST one (a frame with somebody at the camera: 25ms, was 88ms and found nothing) while an empty frame pays both rungs, about 138ms against 88ms, on face-rule cameras only and once per sampling interval. Coverage goes from 7-20% to 7-74%. ALSO FIXED, the second half of the same failure: ONE decision - 'is this the enrolled person?' - was being made twice against different numbers. The worker attaches a name at cosine >= 0.40 and SFace's documented same-identity point is ~0.36, but infra/vision/face.go demanded 0.60, so every genuine match in between was named by the worker and thrown away here. Measured on one subject enrolled from a passport photo and seen on a 1080p camera, 2 of 7 ordinary views landed in that band (backlit at the camera 0.50, backlit across the room 0.59): on a 'known' rule they produced no alert at all, and on an 'unknown' rule they were WORSE THAN NOTHING - an enrolled person reported as a stranger. The default is now the worker's floor, 0.40. Proven through the worker's own live stage against the real gallery: a face at 150/320/450/650px in a 1080p frame is now found and NAMED at every distance, where the last three found nothing at all before. New infra/vision/face_test.go pins that this side is never stricter than the worker, covers all three match modes against the candidate shape the worker emits (including that an enrolled person must never fire an 'unknown' rule), and pins that a zone-less face rule covers the whole frame - the People screen creates them that way, and a zone-less rule that matched nothing would make every one of them silently inert. bench_face_enrol_framing.py now covers both stages: 12 framings, 5 uploads and 7 camera views.

### Security

- **myidsan**: A live bench of the second-factor and step-up flow (tools/fleetbench/bench_idsan_mfa.py, 65/65) found and fixed two real defects on the identity server. First, POST /api/step-up — the endpoint that takes a password and reports whether it was right, to re-authenticate before the most privileged actions — was the only credential-checking endpoint on the server with no lockout behind it; a bench made twelve wrong guesses in 0.6 seconds, all refused, none throttled. Anyone holding a stolen session cookie could not sign in outright but could grind password candidates against it at wire speed. It now shares the same LoginGuard lockout the sign-in surfaces use, keyed on the session's own account, with the same failed-attempt delay and a login.lockout entry when it engages. Second, five audit actions had been declared since MFA shipped and never written by anything: enrolling a second factor, disabling one, spending a break-glass recovery code, and regenerating the recovery set left no trace at all, and a refused login-time second-factor code was filed as an indistinguishable plain login failure. All five now fire from every surface that can produce them — the SPA and server-rendered login challenges, self-service MFA management, step-up, and the WebAuthn re-prove gate. Separately, the RESET_MFA boot-marker escape hatch used to run before the audit service was even constructed, so clearing the stock superadmin's second factor with nobody signing in left only an application-log line; it is now sequenced after the trail exists and writes an mfa.admin_reset entry naming the marker.
- **core,myidsan**: The first live bench of myidsan's brute-force lockout against a real two-instance CLUSTER (two myidsan containers on one shared Postgres and one shared Redis, the shipped configuration) found and fixed two real defects. First, the failed-login lockout (domain/shared/apis.LoginGuard) was purely per-process, so on a clustered deployment - myidsan is documented as genuinely clusterable, and has a wizard step and a Settings panel for declaring it - an attacker's effective budget was maxAttempts times the instance count, and a user locked out on one instance could simply be routed to another and sign in: the bench watched instance A lock an account after eight wrong passwords, then instance B evaluate a ninth normally and go on to accept the CORRECT password with 200. Fixed with a new LoginGuard.WithSharedStore(store, namespace), which mirrors lockout state into the shared cache alongside the existing in-memory map rather than replacing it - a locked verdict is the OR of the two, so the shared half can only ever lock more, never less, and an unreachable cache degrades the guard to exactly today's per-process behaviour rather than disabling brute-force protection. Wired in apps/myidsan/app/app.go whenever cache.provider is redis/redis-cluster. Second, three authenticated endpoints that re-check the signed-in caller's own password had no throttle at all, making each a password oracle for anyone holding a stolen session cookie: POST /api/login/default/change-password (worst - a correct guess there replaces the password and takes the account permanently), DELETE /api/mfa/webauthn/{id} (its identity re-proof accepts the password alone, no second factor required), and DELETE /api/mfa (defence in depth only, since its code gate already fires first). All three now go through new selfThrottleLocked/selfThrottleFailure helpers keyed on the session's own account, so a stranger cannot aim the counter at somebody else. Also fixed along the way: infra/cache.MemoryStore.Delete did not clear a key's sliding-window rate-limit state (kept in a separate map from the cached value), so it meant something different there than on Redis, where the window IS the key - found because the lockout clears a key exactly that way on a successful sign-in.
- **core,myseliasan**: The first live bench of myseliasan's sign-in surface, against a real two-instance CLUSTER (two myseliasan containers on one shared Postgres and one shared Redis, the SHIPPED posture with the rate limiter on), found that the fleet control plane had NO failed-login lockout at all and no audit trail for authentication. POST /api/auth/local-login called AuthenticateLocal and returned: the only brute-force control on the console that owns every camera in the estate was the generic rate limiter - 30 requests a minute, per source, per path, PER INSTANCE, refilling forever, never escalating, blind to one account being attacked from many addresses. The bench served 13 consecutive guesses at roughly 61ms each and then signed in with the correct password as though nothing had happened. POST /api/auth/change-password was the same shape one layer in, an unmetered password oracle for anyone holding a stolen session cookie - 11 current-password guesses went through it in a row. Both now go through the shared sharedapis.LoginGuard, wired from the same loginSecurity config block every other app reads (the block is ABSENT from this app's config.json and an absent block resolves to ON with the suite defaults, so the configuration had been promising a lockout the code never implemented), keyed on ip:<remote addr> AND user:<lowercased username>, with the per-failure delay applied, and attached to the shared cache under namespace myseliasan whenever cache.provider is redis/redis-cluster so the lockout spans the cluster rather than giving an attacker a fresh allowance per instance. A rejected NEW password on change-password is a policy refusal and is deliberately not counted, so a user holding a valid session cannot lock themselves out picking a password the policy keeps refusing. Second finding: the audit trail had never recorded a single authentication event. It records node adoptions, policy edits, key rotations and failover takeovers, so a complete brute-force run left nothing at all in the product's own record of who did what. It now writes login.success, login.failure, login.lockout (once, on the attempt that engages it) and password.change, using the same action names myidsan uses so the two trails correlate, and never the password attempted. On these entries clientIp is the PEER address rather than the X-Forwarded-For the app's other entries use - on a login entry the address is the evidence, and a forged header would also make the trail disagree with the lockout about who was attacking; a proxy's claim is kept in metadata as claimedForwardedFor, labelled as claimed rather than observed. Third finding, and the reason a checklist did not catch any of this: LoginGuard.SharesState() was added in the previous change with a comment saying the clustering preflight reads it to tell an operator the truth about their cluster, and nothing ever read it. The preflight now has a loginLockout blocker row (CheckLoginLockout, domain/shared/services/deployment.go) reporting both that a guard is switched on and that its counters are shared, with a translated label and fix hint in all four languages. Finally, the key derivation, the lockout 429 body and the loginSecurity mapping moved into domain/shared/apis (LoginGuardKeys, LoginGuardSourceKey, LoginGuardAccountKey, WriteLockoutJSON, NewLoginGuardFor) and myidsan's private copies now delegate to them, so there is one implementation of this security decision rather than two that can drift. The bench scores 36/36 against the fix and 17/36 against unfixed main, measured on one identical set of checks (KOPIV2_SKIP_BUILD=1 against a binary built from a worktree at main). A separate screen check (tools/fleetbench/uicheck_sel_lockout.js) drives the real sign-in form in headless Chrome with real keystrokes and confirms a locked-out operator is shown a translated countdown rather than the generic 'invalid username or password' - 4/4 in English, 6/6 in Malay and 6/6 in Arabic including RTL - because the server sends one English sentence and the SPA ships in four languages, so that half is invisible to any API assertion.
- **core,myidsan,myseliasan**: The first live bench of myidsan's session administration, driven across TWO REAL PROCESSES (myidsan plus a relying myseliasan, the whole authorization-code flow, a real non-administrator account), found two real shipped defects. First, sessions issued by the SERVER-RENDERED login page at /api/auth/login were never indexed - and that page is where every relying app's SSO redirect lands, so it is how nearly everybody actually signs in. The bench signed a user in end to end and then found both the user's own session list and the administrator's listing for that account EMPTY while the user was demonstrably signed in at the relying app; revoking answered {"ok":true,"revoked":0} - success, having done nothing. The social-login callback (setOAuthSession, Google/GitHub) had the same gap, so an account that signed in with Google could not be signed out by an administrator at all. Only the JSON local login indexed what it issued. Fixed by pre-minting the session id and indexing on all three paths through one shared helper (apps/myidsan/apis/session_index.go) rather than a third copy. Second, and larger: revoking a session at the identity server did not end it AT THE RELYING APP. myidsan and a relying app are separate processes with separate caches; the relying app is handed myidsan's session id and then mints its own token under its own key, cached under its own TTL - three days by default. The bench watched an administrator revoke an account's sessions, saw the session go 401 at myidsan, and watched the same browser cookie keep working at the fleet console with full access. /api/sso/introspect exists for exactly this and is called by nothing - and could not have been used anyway, since it validates a TOKEN with myidsan's key and the relying app holds its own. Fixed with POST /api/auth/session-status, keyed on the SESSION ID both sides already share and authenticated with the client_id/client_secret pair the relying app already used to redeem its authorization code, so it needs no new configuration on either side; plus a RevocationChecker in the shared auth middleware that re-asks on a TTL and, on a definite negative, deletes the local session entry so every later request is refused with no further round trips. myseliasan wires it using the same CA-trusting HTTP client as the token exchange. The interval is sso.policyCacheTtlSeconds (30s), a config knob that already existed and until now drove nothing at all. The bench scores 27/27 against the fix and 18/27 against unfixed main on one identical set of checks, and MEASURES the propagation window rather than asserting an instant that was never promised: 30.7s against a 30s configured interval.
- **myiotsan,infra**: Live bench of myiotsan's active network discovery scan (tools/fleetbench/bench_iotsan_discovery.py, 38 checks) against the four safety claims infra/iot/discover's package doc makes for every scanner it ships: READ-ONLY, LAN-local, bounded, cancellable - plus that a scan proposes candidates and never adds a device, that it is opt-in/admin-only, and that it is audited. A new modbus_tripwire.py stands up a real Modbus TCP device that answers as a plausible SunSpec device and records the function code of every request, splitting reads from writes, and deliberately accepts a write with a success reply (rather than an exception) so a scanner falling back to a read path on failure cannot hide the evidence; a Blackhole socket that accepts and never answers is what actually exercises a per-host timeout, since a closed port fails instantly and proves nothing. The bench found one real defect: LAN-local was stated in the package doc but never enforced. The multicast scanners (mDNS/SSDP/EtherNet-IP/BACnet) are link-local by construction, but the Modbus sweep takes its target from a CIDR string an admin types, and nothing checked it - a running appliance accepted and really ran a sweep of 192.0.2.0/24 (public TEST-NET-1 address space, 6.4s) and of 127.0.0.1/32 (the appliance's own loopback), making the scan endpoint a general-purpose port scanner pointed wherever the appliance has a route. Fixed in discover.Hosts, the one funnel every sweep target passes through: a new checkLANLocal/isLANLocal pair refuses anything outside RFC 1918 + RFC 6598 CGNAT + RFC 3927 IPv4 link-local + IPv6 ULA/link-local, and refuses loopback, unspecified and multicast addresses outright. A bare hostname is resolved and every address it yields must qualify; an unresolvable target is refused rather than scanned (fail closed); a list with one bad target among good ones is refused whole; CIDR bounds are checked before expansion so a wide public range is refused on sight. Five new unit tests in infra/iot/discover/discover_test.go. After the fix the refusal is instant (0.0s) where the sweep used to cost 6.4s, and a genuine LAN range still scans normally. Everything else in the safety posture held and is now proven live rather than merely designed that way: the entire scan against the tripwire issued exactly four requests, all function code 3 (read holding registers), and no write ever arrived; a /8 is refused before any probe goes out; a stalling host is bounded by the per-op timeout; abandoning the request mid-scan stops further dialling; a scan produces candidates and adds no device; operator and viewer are both refused the scan and window endpoints; and a scan is recorded in the notification feed. Also documented as an honest limit, not a defect: the scan is synchronous and holds its HTTP request open for its whole duration (measured up to 41.7s at the full host cap with all five scanners selected), which only completes because myiotsan's shipped config disables the write timeout - noted on ScanService's own doc comment so the host cap and that timeout are never tuned independently. 38/38 with the fix; 36/38 against the unfixed app.
## 2026-08-26 — myidsan 1.46.0, myiotsan 0.30.0, mymatasan 1.143.0, myseliasan 1.78.0 (fc6b5cc)

### Security

- **myidsan,myiotsan,mymatasan,myseliasan**: Cleared every outstanding security advisory in the web interface dependencies — 34 of them across all five apps and the product website, fourteen rated high. Most were build-time tooling rather than anything a user's browser ever runs, but a few (the routing library, an image-processing library, an HTTP client) do end up in shipped code, and leaving known advisories open is not a position worth defending. Three libraries needed a major version step and one was simply deleted: the door-controller app carried a routing library it never once imported, and the best fix for a vulnerable dependency is not upgrading it. Nothing about how any app looks or behaves changes.
## 2026-08-25 — mymatasan 1.142.0, myseliasan 1.77.0 (58a25f6)

### Added

- **myseliasan**: A failover plan now tells you whether the spare can actually carry the cameras, not just whether it can reach them. Testing a plan used to answer one question — can the standby appliance open these cameras — and stayed silent on the other one, which is whether it could record them all at once. The control plane now asks the spare itself how many cameras it reckons it can handle, counts the ones it is already recording of its own, adds anything other plans have pointed at the same spare, and says plainly whether it fits, only just fits, or does not. A plan that does not fit will not be called ready, however well its test went. What it will still do is take the cameras over anyway when a recorder dies: being over the estimate means dropped frames, and nothing recording at all is the one outcome that cannot be undone afterwards — so you are told, loudly, in the alert and in the trail, and the takeover proceeds.
- **mymatasan**: Anything in the notification feed can now be put straight into a case file, from the screen where you noticed it. Until now the only route was to read the time off the row, go to the timeline, find the moment again and bookmark it — which loses any record of what you were looking at and takes long enough that it mostly did not happen. Every row now has an Add to case button, and the recorder works out for itself what the entry actually refers to: an entry about an AI detection goes in as that detection, with its own camera, time and picture, so the same piece of evidence can never end up in a case twice under two different names. An entry about a camera keeps the footage either side of the moment, and the case holds it against retention like any other evidence. And an entry about the machine itself — a restart, a disk filling — goes in too, with no footage, because that is often the fact that explains the gap in the recording either side of it. Your note goes in with it either way.
- **myseliasan**: The control plane can now run a video wall that spans recorders. Pick cameras from any appliance in the fleet, arrange them in a grid, give the wall a name, and it is saved centrally — the same wall for everybody who opens the screen, rather than something each person rebuilds in their own browser at the start of every shift. It rotates through its pages on its own if you ask it to, and one wall can be marked the one a screen opens with. The part a single recorder cannot do at all: when a camera on the wall raises an alert, the wall shows that camera and stops rotating until you have had time to look — and the alert can come from any building, because this is the only thing that sees all of them. Tiles are honest about what they cannot show: a camera on an appliance that is offline says so in words instead of going black, which matters because a black rectangle looks exactly like a dark room, and one on an appliance that has left the fleet says that instead, because they are different problems. A tile is never quietly dropped from an arrangement.
## 2026-08-25 — myseliasan 1.75.1 (e6697b6)

### Fixed

- **myseliasan**: The fleet compliance screen was reporting appliances as compliant with policies that had been deleted. Remove the last policy and every node kept its green tick — on the page and through the API — until somebody happened to press Check now, which meant an estate governed by nothing could look like an estate in perfect order. It now says what is actually true: with no policy in force, every node reads as having no policy, which is honest rather than healthy. And because a comparison takes a round trip to every appliance and is deliberately something you ask for rather than something a page load does behind your back, editing or adding a policy no longer leaves the previous verdicts looking current — the screen now says plainly that they were reached before your change and offers to compare again. Also fixed: a policy covering everything described itself as "Whole fleet: whole fleet".
## 2026-08-25 — myseliasan 1.75.0, core 1.105.0 (1ec2a05)

### Added

- **core,myseliasan**: The control plane can now wake a phone. Add the fleet page to your home screen and it installs like an app; register a device and this control plane will raise a notification on it when the fleet needs attention, even with the page closed and the screen off. Each device gets its own threshold, deliberately: the phone in somebody's pocket at three in the morning and the laptop on their desk want different answers, and one setting for the whole site either wakes everybody for everything or wakes nobody for anything. What the feature will not do is claim to work. Registering a device sends a real notification there and then, and the screen reports what actually happened rather than that a switch is on — including "never tested", which is shown as its own state because it is not the same as working. If no push service could be reached, it says so in plain words and names the hosts the machine would need to talk to, and it says that this is the expected answer on a site with no internet access rather than leaving somebody to hunt a fault that is not there. Every device shows what its last real attempt did and when, and a device that has been reinstalled is removed on its own instead of being retried forever. Also fixed: the fleet has been raising an alarm when a site goes dark and never once telling anybody when it came back — the all-clear existed, but the way a node actually returns meant it could never fire.
## 2026-08-25 — mymatasan 1.141.0 (9fd9546)

### Added

- **mymatasan**: An evidence export can now be asked to destroy the faces in it. Every frame is scanned, and each face found is filled with a solid black box — widened beyond the face and held for the frames either side of it, so a detector that drops a face for a moment does not leave a full-resolution one visible in the middle of a clip nobody will scrub through. It works alongside the existing privacy-zone redaction: a copy can hide the neighbour's window, the faces, or both, and the manifest names each of them separately. The thing the product will not do is overstate it. A privacy zone is a guarantee — a person drew it, it does not move, and the export covers it. Hiding faces is a best effort against an automatic detector that misses faces which are turned away, distant, partly hidden or blurred by motion, and the product says so beside the checkbox before you make the copy, again beside the finished file, and in the bundle's own manifest. It also reports what actually happened: how many frames were scanned, how many detections were destroyed, and how far beyond each face it painted — and it says in plain words that a count of detections is not a count of people. An appliance that cannot do it refuses the export rather than handing back a copy that hid nothing.
## 2026-08-25 — mymatasan 1.140.0, myseliasan 1.74.0, core 1.104.0 (89e440b)

### Added

- **core,mymatasan,myseliasan**: A recorder is the only thing recording its own cameras, and when it stops — a dead power supply, a failed disk, a switch port, somebody carrying it out of the building — nothing anywhere starts recording them again. Now you can name a spare appliance for a recorder, and while that recorder is healthy the spare is quietly given a copy of its camera list so it can pick those cameras up. The part that matters is the part you can press on a quiet afternoon: Test asks the spare to actually open every one of those cameras and reports, per camera, whether it could. Until you have done that, the plan says NEVER TESTED and nothing on the screen says otherwise — copying only proves the two appliances can talk to each other, and whether the spare can reach the cameras themselves is a different question with a different answer. When a recorder does go quiet for longer than a wait you choose, the control plane tells you it is ready to fail over, or — if you have deliberately armed it after your own test passed — takes the cameras over by itself. Either way you are told per camera what actually happened, read back from the recorder rather than assumed: a camera that is recording says so, and one whose stream could not be opened says that instead of being counted as a success. Handing the cameras back afterwards is one button, and the footage the spare recorded during the outage stays on the spare, playable, including after the plan itself is deleted. Camera logins are moved between the two appliances in a sealed envelope the control plane cannot open and that cannot be opened by any appliance except the spare it was addressed to.
## 2026-08-24 — mymatasan 1.139.0, core 1.103.0 (70ca00d)

### Added

- **core,mymatasan**: You can now mark parts of a camera's view that must not be seen — the window next door, the pavement outside the gate, the keypad somebody types a PIN into. Draw the area on the picture, using the same editor the detection zones use, and it is protected two ways at once. Where the camera can do it, the recorder asks the camera to black the area out ITSELF, which means those pixels are never recorded at all: not on the disk, not in an export, and not on a drive somebody walks off with. And whatever the camera can do, you can now tick a box when exporting footage to have those areas blacked out of the copy you hand over. The two are genuinely different promises, so the screen tells you which one you have got, per camera, in plain words rather than leaving you to assume the stronger one. It also does not take the camera's word for it: cameras are known to accept a setting and quietly keep doing something else, so the recorder reads the masks back off the camera and compares them with what it asked for. If they do not match — a camera that stored a different shape, or nothing at all — the screen says the area is NOT confirmed as hidden and tells you to treat the recording as containing it, naming the area in question. A camera that cannot hide anything at all says so plainly instead of pretending.
## 2026-08-24 — mymatasan 1.138.0, core 1.102.0 (def29f4)

### Added

- **core,mymatasan**: Your cameras can now tell the recorder what THEY noticed, and the recorder can switch things on. Most cameras have a terminal block with a couple of inputs and one or two relay outputs, and until now this product ignored both. Wire a door contact, a beam across a gateway, a PIR or a panic button under a counter into a camera's input and it appears in the notification feed the moment it trips, named — 'a device wired to Loading bay (input 1) is active' — filterable to that camera, and routable to email, webhook, Telegram or MQTT like any other alert. It is filed as a sensor reading rather than as an AI detection, so you can send door contacts to the people who care about doors without also sending them every person the AI sees. The other direction is the one that changes what the product is for: an output can drive a siren, a strobe, a gate, a door strike or a light, from a button beside the camera or automatically when a detection rule fires. That is the difference between recording an intrusion and responding to one. Switching something on briefly is the normal gesture, and the recorder hands the job of switching it back off to the CAMERA wherever the camera will take it, so a power cut or a restart here cannot leave a siren sounding; where a camera refuses, the screen says plainly that the recorder is the one holding it. A detection rule can only ever switch an output on briefly, never latch it — the rule that would leave a siren sounding is the one firing at four in the morning with nobody watching. And the button that switches something OFF is never refused, never disabled and never greyed out, not even while the recorder is busy doing something else: an appliance that can decline to stop a siren is worse than one that cannot start one.
## 2026-08-24 — mymatasan 1.137.0, core 1.101.0 (0d1317e)

### Added

- **core,mymatasan**: A PTZ camera can now be told WHERE to look, not just which way to nudge. Save a position on the camera under a name you choose — Front gate, Loading bay — and send the camera there with one press, from the same place you drive it. Set a home position and send it back. Build a guard tour: an ordered route of those positions, with however long you want the camera to hold each one, running on its own and coming back after a power cut without anybody restarting it. And point a camera at trouble automatically: any detection rule can name a saved position, so a rule that spots someone at the fence swings the dome onto the gate and holds it there while somebody looks. The saved positions live ON THE CAMERA, where the camera's own web page put them, so the two can never disagree about where it can point — and if a position is deleted from the camera, the tour that used it says so instead of quietly skipping it. When three things want the same camera at once, the order is fixed and deliberate: the person at the controls always wins, because they are watching something the appliance cannot see; an alarm outranks the patrol; and the patrol runs when nobody else wants the camera. An alarm arriving while you are driving the camera is reported to you rather than taking it out of your hands. A patrol that can no longer walk its route — because its positions were deleted from the camera — stops and raises an alert, rather than reporting itself as running while nothing moves.
## 2026-08-24 — mymatasan 1.136.0, core 1.100.0 (94b2d4a)

### Added

- **core,mymatasan**: Three new detection rules that ask a question no single frame can answer. LOITERING alerts when a person (or whatever you choose) has been inside a zone longer than you allow. LEFT BEHIND alerts when a bag, case or box stops moving and stays put — and, by default, only when nobody is standing beside it, because a bag with its owner next to it is not abandoned and a rule that alerted on every waiting passenger would be switched off within a day. DIRECTION OF TRAVEL alerts when something crosses a zone heading a particular way: the wrong-way rule, with directions relative to the picture rather than to the compass. None of them adds a second AI pass. They read the same detections and the same object tracking the existing line-crossing rules use, so a camera already running detection pays nothing extra to gain them. The care went into the difference between a rule about a moment and a rule about a stretch of time. A frame rule that is wrong for one frame is corrected by the next one; a time rule that is wrong for one frame loses its clock, and a thirty-second threshold then never fires on a real camera where people walk behind pillars. So a missed detection is forgiven for a few passes, while being SEEN somewhere else is not: stepping out of the zone restarts the clock immediately, because that is information rather than the absence of it. The forgiveness is counted in missed detections rather than in seconds, so it means the same thing on a camera sampled once a second and one sampled once a minute. A loitering alert records WHEN THE DWELL BEGAN, not only when the alert fired — the alert arrives at 14:05 and the thirty seconds worth watching start at 14:04:30. And a rule that could never fire is refused at the point of saving rather than accepted and left silent: no object classes, or a direction rule with no direction, are both errors that say what is missing.
## 2026-08-24 — mymatasan 1.135.0 (e75be00)

### Added

- **mymatasan**: Live View becomes a video wall. An arrangement worth keeping is now a WALL: a name, a grid, and the cameras in the order you put them in, saved on the appliance instead of in the browser. That one change is most of the feature — the old arrangement lived in a cookie, so it existed only on the machine that built it, could not be handed to the operator on the next shift, could not be opened on a second monitor without being rebuilt by hand, and vanished when somebody cleared their browser. Walls are shared rather than personal, because a control room's walls are furniture and an administrator has to be able to fix the one everybody is looking at; one can be marked as the wall a screen opens with. Two numbers decide what a wall does while nobody is touching it. CYCLE advances through the pages every few seconds, so a station can watch more cameras than fit the grid. POP brings a camera onto the visible page when it raises an alert and holds it there, outlined, so it is obvious which camera the wall jumped for — and cycling pauses while it is showing, because a wall that rotates away two seconds later has done the opposite of raising an alarm. Paging by hand restarts the dwell, so a page you turned to is not whipped away a moment later. SECOND MONITOR opens the selected wall in its own window with no navigation and nothing else on screen, to be dragged onto another display and left there. It is a real browser window because a panel inside the app cannot be dragged to another screen. A wall that names a camera somebody has since deleted says so, rather than quietly rendering one tile fewer and letting an operator believe they are watching everything.
## 2026-08-24 — mymatasan 1.134.0, core 1.99.0 (8009f6e)

### Added

- **core,mymatasan**: An investigation now has somewhere to live. A CASE FILE collects the footage, the recorded sightings and the operator's notes for one incident in one place: bookmark the moment you are watching straight off the Timeline (on every camera on screen at once, which is what an incident usually looks like), add a sighting from Object Search, annotate each piece of evidence with why it matters, hand the case to a colleague, and close it with a stated outcome that is required rather than optional. The whole case exports as ONE verifiable file: every clip joined without re-encoding, a manifest naming each source segment and its SHA-256 alongside any period inside a clip that has no recording, the case's own audit trail as a chain-of-custody CSV, and a plain-text note explaining how to check all of it with standard tools. If a piece of evidence has no footage left, the bundle is still produced and that clip is listed as missing with the reason, because an export that quietly dropped it would look complete. The part that makes a case worth opening rather than a memo: WHILE A CASE IS OPEN, THE FOOTAGE ITS EVIDENCE POINTS AT IS NOT DELETED. Retention, the per-camera 'Purge now' and the automatic clean-up that runs when the disk fills all refuse to destroy it, including the recorder's own file sweep, which deletes by filename and knows nothing about the database. On an appliance keeping seven days of video, a case opened today would otherwise be a list of broken links next week, and nothing about the case would have changed to say so. The case screen states what it is holding, how much disk that is, and how many of those clips are only still present BECAUSE the case is open; closing the case restates that number before it releases them. Secure wipe, factory reset and deleting a camera still destroy footage regardless — a case is not a lock against the operations whose whole purpose is destruction. A second, unrelated gap this closed: the operator role could START an evidence export and could not download it. Bundles are built asynchronously, and the grant carried POST only, so the capability the role model describes as 'may export footage, may not delete it' was unusable for the role it was written for. Benching this against a recorder holding real footage turned up four more things worth stating, three of which were fixes to code that already shipped. An evidence bundle is whole stored recordings joined together, so asking for eighteen seconds hands over the minute they sit inside — and until now nothing in the bundle said so, leaving anyone who read timestamps by counting from the first frame of the video wrong by the difference. The footage is still not cut (a cut lands on the nearest keyframe rather than the moment asked for, and handing over less than was recorded is the worse answer), but every bundle now states when its video actually starts and ends, how many seconds it holds, and how far into the file the requested moment begins, in the manifest and in plain words in the verification note. The chain of custody now ends with the export itself rather than one event before it. A case bundle can no longer be collected through the single-clip download route, which a different permission governs, and export links are no longer guessable from the time they were created. And a piece of evidence bookmarked across the moment a camera came back — some of it recorded, some of it before recording began — is no longer reported as having no footage at all; it plays from the first frame that exists and says when the recording starts.
## 2026-08-23 — mymatasan 1.133.0, myseliasan 1.73.0, core 1.98.0 (4e820d9)

### Added

- **core,mymatasan,myseliasan**: An operator can now pick a recorded person or vehicle and ask 'where else did this go?'. When appearance search is switched on for a camera (a new per-camera setting, separate from and requiring object metadata recording), the detector attaches a compact appearance descriptor to eligible person/vehicle detections. It combines a general-purpose image embedding, sharing the same backbone the taught-anomaly feature already loads, with a colour and lightness profile taken from the upper and lower halves of each figure — so a search for the person in the red jacket is answered by something that can actually see the jacket. No additional model is downloaded or shipped: a purpose-trained re-identification network would match better across very different camera angles, but the published ones are trained on research-only datasets, one of which was withdrawn by its own authors over consent concerns, and that is not something to build a sold product on. The metadata recorder keeps the descriptor from each sighting's clearest frame and stores it, encrypted at rest, alongside (not inside) the searchable object index. A new 'Find similar' action on any Object Search result ranks every other recorded sighting by how much it looks like the one picked, and myseliasan's control plane can now ask the same question across every reachable node in a fleet, fetching the query sighting's descriptor from the node that recorded it before fanning it out. The ranking is deliberately never shown as a match percentage: measured against the real model, two photos of the same person and two photos of different people score almost the same raw similarity, so the screen instead reports how far a result STANDS OUT from everything else compared for that search (a robust, self-calibrating score), and says plainly when there were too few sightings to compare against for that to mean anything. This is a ranked shortlist for a human to confirm by eye, never an identification. A second, unrelated gap this closed: the per-camera 'Purge now' action used to delete a camera's footage and AI-event snapshots but leave its searchable object index (and now, appearance descriptors) intact and pointing at nothing — it now purges all three together.
## 2026-08-23 — mymatasan 1.132.0 (e5493d5)

### Added

- **mymatasan**: Added a Timeline playback screen (Workspace nav, beside Live Views) that plays recorded footage by the clock instead of by file: a scrub bar shaded from the same merged footage spans the coverage strip reports, seeking across segment boundaries automatically, up to 8 cameras synchronised against one moment, playback speed 0.25x-8x, and detection events plotted as clickable jump marks. Backed by two new endpoints, GET /api/recording/timeline (segment index + coverage over a window) and GET /api/recording/timeline/seek (resolve one wall-clock moment to a playable segment + offset per camera, snapping forward and reporting the gap when the moment isn't covered), both capped at 8 cameras and a 31-day window per request and refusing rather than truncating a camera with too many segments in range. The coverage-span merge was factored out of the existing coverage service so the bar, the coverage percentages, and the continuity monitor can never disagree, and the covering-segment resolver object search already used for sighting playback was lifted to a shared function so Timeline seek and Object Search agree on which file covers a given moment.
## 2026-08-23 — myidsan 1.45.0, mymatasan 1.131.0, myseliasan 1.72.0, core 1.97.0 (124597f)

### Added

- **core,mymatasan,myseliasan,myidsan**: Notifications can now be delivered by email. Alerts, health events and system notices could already be sent to a webhook, a Telegram chat or an MQTT broker; each of those needs something an organisation has to set up and keep running, while a mailbox is something every organisation already operates, audits and retains. Email is now a delivery destination alongside the others, with its own recipient list, its own subject prefix so a reader covering several sites can tell them apart, its own severity floor, and the same choice about whether the alert image is attached or merely referenced. The mail server itself is configured once for the whole installation rather than once per recipient list, so there is a single place that decides whether this installation talks to a mail server at all, and a single password to change when it changes. Separately, and more consequentially, the fleet control plane could not send anything anywhere at all. It recorded every event, wrote it to its log and pushed it to any browser that happened to be open, and stopped there — so an operator responsible for fifty sites only learned that one had gone dark if they were looking at the screen when it happened, which is rarely the case at three in the morning. The control plane now delivers outbound like the appliances do, by email and also through the webhook and Telegram settings that had been present in its configuration file all along without ever being used. It also gains a button that sends a real test message through the configured mail server, so the mail path can be proved to work before an incident depends on it, rather than after.
## 2026-08-23 — myiotsan 0.29.0, mymatasan 1.130.0, myseliasan 1.71.0, core 1.96.0 (3c67396)

### Added

- **core,mymatasan,myiotsan,myseliasan**: Events a node could not send to the control plane are now counted instead of vanishing. A node forwards every alert, health event and system notice up its control channel as it happens; when that channel is down the event is not queued, and the control plane recovers it on reconnect by replaying the node's own record. That recovery works — but until now nothing counted what was missed, so a node whose events were disappearing and a node with nothing to say produced exactly the same telemetry: none. Nodes now count what they could not send, separated by what was lost and by why (the channel was down, or the send itself failed), alongside what they did send, so the number can be judged against a total. A healthy node reports zero rather than reporting nothing at all. On reconnecting, a node tells the control plane how many events it could not send while it was away — which is precisely what the replay that follows is about to go and fetch, so the recovery can be checked rather than merely assumed, and it appears in the operator's feed. Separately, the control plane now watches the clock on that recovery. Missed events can only be replayed from the last 72 hours of a node's own record, so a long outage eventually passes the point where they are gone for good — and nothing marked that moment; a node offline for two hours and one offline for four days were described identically. Nodes are now flagged two-thirds of the way through that window, while there is still a day in which to act, and again once it has passed, saying exactly when recovery stopped being possible. A new fleet endpoint reports where every node stands. Finally, the reconnect replay had a ceiling of 25,000 events and stopped there without a word: a replay that recovered part of what was missed reported itself exactly like one that recovered all of it. It now says so.
## 2026-08-22 — mymatasan 1.129.0, myseliasan 1.70.0 (237a88f)

### Added

- **mymatasan,myseliasan**: A fleet can now be upgraded a few appliances at a time instead of all at once. Plan a rollout to a specific version and the control plane divides the estate into RINGS — a canary first — asks each ring to install that exact version over the existing control channel, and refuses to start the next ring until the current one has proved itself. Proving itself means three things, not one: every node in the ring came back on the control channel, REPORTED the target version itself, and held that state for a settle window. Those catch three different ways an upgrade goes wrong — a node that never returns, a node that returns still running the old build because the swap silently failed, and a node that boots, looks perfectly healthy, and falls over a minute later. The control plane can judge any of this because it now records what each appliance says it is running, captured from the control-channel greeting that already carried it. Nodes that cannot replace their own binary at all — a container image, a package-managed install — are identified while the rollout is being PLANNED, listed with the way they are actually upgraded instead, and left out of the rings rather than discovered by a canary that fails on one. The rollout screen for all of this is the API and the audit trail; every plan, start, halt and completion is recorded.
## 2026-08-22 — mymatasan 1.128.0, myseliasan 1.69.0 (75faf23)

### Added

- **mymatasan,myseliasan**: The fleet can now be asked one question at a time instead of one appliance at a time. A new federated search on the control plane asks every node an operator can reach — in parallel, over the existing mTLS control channel — what it saw, and merges the answers newest-first into a single list: object classes the AI detected, and the plates and people it recognized, narrowed by node, site, object, plate or person text, time window and confidence. Recognized identities do not live in the object index, so each node answers from both: presence intervals from its metadata recorder, and the license-plate and face identities recorded on its alerts. Results carry the node, the site and the CAMERA'S OWN NAME, because every recorder numbers its cameras from one and "camera 3" means a different place on each. Objects are searched under the node's Objects grant and identities under its AI-log grant, so no role can reach through the fleet for data it could not read on the appliance itself, and every estate-wide search is written to the audit trail with the terms it used. The Objects screen now shows a coverage line above the results on every search — which nodes answered, which did not and why, and how far back the answer is actually complete. That line is the feature: the browser used to fan out to each node itself and silently swallow the ones that failed, so a recorder that had been unreachable for a week simply contributed no rows, and an investigator reading an empty table could not tell "the fleet never saw it" from "the half of the fleet that would have seen it was never asked".
## 2026-08-22 — mymatasan 1.127.0, myseliasan 1.68.0, core 1.95.0 (3da546e)

### Added

- **core,mymatasan,myseliasan**: The fleet can now keep its own copy of the footage that matters, off the appliance. A new per-rule 'Keep a fleet copy' flag on a mymatasan detection rule (default off) marks the alerts whose evidence must survive the recorder being stolen, burned, submerged or wiped by whoever set off the alarm — the failure modes that destroy the recording of the very event it is evidence of, and the one case that retention, tamper detection and continuity monitoring all assume cannot happen. When such a rule fires, the flag rides upstream with the alert; the control plane queues the clip, waits for the node to finish cutting it, then pulls it over the existing mutual-TLS control tunnel in bounded byte ranges, hashes the bytes as they arrive, and stores the footage and its snapshot encrypted at rest beside the incident record. A new read-only /api/clips surface lists what the fleet has kept and plays it back with seeking, publishing the digest so a recipient can verify the copy; every viewing is written to the audit trail, because once the fleet holds a copy the appliance's own trail can no longer answer who watched it. Deliberately narrow: nothing is archived unless a rule was explicitly flagged, since a control plane holding every clip from every camera is a storage problem pretending to be a security feature. Deliberately careful too — a clip that arrives short is refused rather than stored (a truncated video still plays, so nothing downstream would ever notice the missing thirty seconds with the incident in them), a snapshot is kept only if it really is an image, a clip the fleet was asked to keep and could not raises an alert rather than failing quietly, and an archive that reaches its size limit stops and says so rather than deleting older evidence to make room.
## 2026-08-21 — myseliasan 1.67.0 (3f34d2e)

### Added

- **myseliasan**: myseliasan can now answer what the fleet's uptime WAS, not only what it is. A new append-only node state history records every transition of a node's liveness (online / lost / self-dropped), written by the four paths that legitimately change it — the heartbeat reconciler, adoption, a node dialing in on the control channel, and the authenticated self-drop notice — one row per change, so a node up for a year costs one row. A companion table records the spans during which the control plane ITSELF was not watching (a crash, a reboot, a long upgrade, or a leadership handover), detected from a sweep watermark rather than declared, so an unclean shutdown is covered like any other. From those, availability is reported per node, per site and per calendar month over any past window, via a new GET /api/nodes/availability endpoint and a new Availability section in the fleet health PDF — which replaces that report's own footnote admitting historical uptime was not tracked. Three rules keep the figures honest rather than flattering: time nothing was watching is subtracted from the denominator and reported separately instead of counted as uptime; a node with no measured time reads 'no data', never 100%; and availability is floored, never rounded, so 98.999% reads 98.99 and only zero downtime prints 100.00%. Site and month rollups aggregate node-seconds rather than averaging per-node percentages, so a node adopted yesterday cannot weigh as heavily on a site's month as one that ran all of it. Outages are dated to the last moment there was contact rather than to the sweep that gave up waiting, so an outage is not silently short by the grace window. Both new tables are included in .selbackup's fleet section, and releasing a node deletes its history so re-adopting the same appliance does not inherit an outage that never happened.
## 2026-08-21 — mymatasan 1.126.2, core 1.94.1 (69c4680)

### Fixed

- **core,mymatasan**: Fixed the camera tamper monitor's MOVED verdict (W1-5, shipped in PR #173), which could never actually fire: it compared each sample's luma histogram against the previous sample instead of against a rolling per-camera reference, so a physically re-aimed camera differed from its predecessor for exactly one sample and then matched it forever, resetting the required consecutive-sample streak before an alert could be raised. infra/vision/tamper.go gains MedianHistogram (per-bucket median of a window of histograms, renormalized to sum to 1) and HistogramDistanceFrom (distance against that reference rather than against another frame); the monitor now keeps a rolling window of recent histograms (tamperState.hists, excluding samples taken while MOVED is already active) the same way it already keeps a rolling edge-energy baseline, and measures MOVED distance against that reference computed before the current sample is folded in. MOVED is suppressed in low light and while COVERED is active or verdict-true, since a covered lens changes the whole histogram too and the picture cannot support judging where the camera is aimed while it cannot see out. 7 new tests cover the regression, the new-normal and recovery cases, the person-crossing and dusk false-positive guards, and the covered-suppresses-moved interaction; 3 new pure-function tests cover MedianHistogram/HistogramDistanceFrom directly.
## 2026-08-20 — myseliasan 1.66.0 (06d8c86)

### Added

- **myseliasan**: myseliasan gains fleet configuration policy + drift detection: a FleetPolicy (fleet/site/node scope, per-node-kind) declares what a whitelisted set of node settings (recording continuity, camera reachability, tamper detection, appliance health thresholds, alert retention) ought to be, and a leader-gated background reconciler compares each adopted node's live settings against the winning policy every 15 minutes, reporting compliant/drifted/unknown/unmanaged per node. Enforcement — writing a drifted value back to the node over the existing control tunnel — is opt-in per policy and defaults off (report-only), with every enforced write audited. New superadmin-gated /api/fleet-policies* endpoints and a Fleet Policy screen. While building this, an unrelated backup gap was found and fixed: FleetRuleClause rows were never exported by the .selbackup 'rules' section, so a restored fleet correlation rule always came back with zero clauses and could never fire; a new 'policies' backup section covers the new entities and the 'rules' section now carries clauses too.
## 2026-08-19 — mymatasan 1.126.1 (95c8b7e)

### Fixed

- **mymatasan**: Evidence export captured the ffmpeg path once at startup instead of resolving it per export. Every other consumer in the app reads that setting live, deliberately — the recorders were changed to do so precisely because a captured copy goes stale. The in-app ffmpeg installer writes this setting at runtime, so an operator who installed ffmpeg through the product and then tried to export footage got a failure that persisted until someone restarted the app: exactly the install where the product had been asked to set ffmpeg up. The export now resolves the path on each run and reports a clear message when none is configured. Found by benching the export against real video for the first time — that bench also confirmed the concat produces a valid, playable H.264 file whose SHA-256 matches the manifest, that the manifest and VERIFY.txt report a real recording gap, and that corrupting a stored segment digest makes the export refuse to run rather than shipping altered footage.
## 2026-08-19 — mymatasan 1.126.0, myseliasan 1.65.1, core 1.94.0 (e9739bb)

### Added

- **myseliasan**: myseliasan can now back itself up. Settings gains a Backup & Restore tab that exports a passphrase-encrypted .selbackup and restores from one. Until now the control plane had factory reset and nothing else, while mymatasan (.mmbackup) and myidsan (.idbackup) both shipped this — and myseliasan is the app holding the state that cannot be rebuilt from anywhere else: the fleet certificate authority's private key, the node registry with each node's heartbeat token, RBAC, sites and floor plans, fleet rules and the audit trail. Losing that database did not degrade the fleet, it orphaned it: every node had to be physically re-adopted with a fresh claim code. Eight selectable sections (access, users, fleetca, fleet, sites, rules, settings, audit) with row counts shown before export. Secrets and the on-disk floor plan images are unsealed on export and RE-SEALED with the destination host's key on restore, so a bundle restores onto a fresh install with a completely new encryption key — which is the disaster it exists for. Restoring the fleet CA reports that a restart is required, because the running process caches the CA in memory and would otherwise keep serving mTLS from the old one and reject every node. The audit section is append-only and ignores replace mode, so restoring an empty backup cannot be used to erase the trail. Superadmin-only, and both export and restore are audited (never with the passphrase).
- **core,mymatasan,myseliasan**: mymatasan now has an audit trail, and all three apps share one implementation. Deleting a recording, purging a camera's footage, changing retention, and viewing or downloading a segment now each record who did it, from where, with what client, and — for a deletion — which camera and time range were lost, captured before the row is removed. Previously mymatasan recorded none of this: it was the only app in the suite with no audit log, and the one holding the actual video. "Who watched this footage" is a mandatory line item in GDPR Article 30 records and in essentially every government CCTV tender, and it cannot be reconstructed after the fact. Camera credential changes (username only, never the password) and user create/update/delete are recorded too. New admin-only routes GET /api/audit and GET /api/audit.csv expose the trail for review and export; there is deliberately no delete or update route, because a trail the recorded party can edit is not a trail. Under this, the audit implementation moved to domain/shared/audit: myidsan and myseliasan had each written their own and the two had already drifted (only one truncated hostile input, only one had retention, only one recorded the user agent), and adding a third copy would have made that worse. myseliasan gains retention, input truncation, the user-agent field, and filtering by outcome, actor and date range; its fleet-health report now applies the date window in the query rather than filtering the newest 500 rows afterwards, which previously let a busy period outside the window push in-window entries off the end of the report.
- **mymatasan**: mymatasan now checks that it actually recorded. A new continuity monitor scores each closed hour of every recording-enabled camera against how much footage is really on disk, and raises a critical 'Recording gap' alert when a camera stops writing — then clears it when recording resumes. Until now nothing answered that question: the camera health monitor probes reachability, and a camera can be perfectly reachable while ffmpeg is wedged, the disk is full, the remux queue is quarantining every segment, or the stream URL silently changed. All of those recorded nothing and reported green, and an operator found out when somebody asked for footage of an incident. The Recordings page gains a coverage strip showing the last 7/30/90 days shaded by how complete each day is, backed by a new GET /api/recording/coverage endpoint — the same read model the monitor scores against, so the screen and the alert can never disagree. Care was taken to make it usable rather than noisy: only whole closed hours are scored, each hour is scored once regardless of sweep frequency, the default minimum is 95% (segment rollover and a recorder restart cost a few seconds an hour legitimately), the disk guard's recording pause suppresses per-camera alerts because it already raises its own, and a gap on a camera the health monitor knows is offline is attributed to that outage rather than reported as a second independent fault. Cameras with recording disabled — including detect-only AI frame sources — are never scored. Tunable from Settings > Camera health; new metrics mymatasan_recording_coverage_percent and mymatasan_recording_gap_cameras.
- **core,mymatasan**: mymatasan can now export footage as a verifiable evidence bundle. Ask for a time range on a camera, state a reason, and get a single .zip containing the joined video, a manifest.json recording where it came from and what it covers, and a plain-text VERIFY.txt explaining how to check it with standard tools. Until now playback and download worked per stored segment only, so producing '14:05 to 14:40 on camera 3' meant downloading several files and stitching them externally — which destroys any claim the footage is unmodified and left no record that it happened. Segments are now SHA-256 hashed at finalize, on the plaintext mp4 before at-rest encryption, so the digest survives a key change, a backup and restore, and a move between hosts. That is what lets a bundle claim the footage was not altered between recording and export; a segment with no stored digest (recorded before this existed, or recovered after a crash when the file was already encrypted) is hashed at export time and labelled 'computed-at-export' instead, because that only proves the file has not changed since the export and must not be presented as the stronger claim. The manifest always lists every gap in the requested range — an export that silently skips missing footage looks continuous, which is worse than refusing to export — and the export dialog shows those gaps before the operator commits. A segment whose stored digest does not match the bytes on disk fails the export rather than being included quietly. Exporting is grantable to operators as a new second level on the Recordings page; deleting footage remains superadmin-only, which is the same line drawn twice: an operator who was present at an incident must be able to hand the footage to somebody and must not be able to destroy it. Every export is audited at both request and download.
- **core,mymatasan**: mymatasan now notices when a camera stops seeing its scene. A new tamper monitor samples the frame the recorder already siphons for the AI detector and raises an alert when the lens is covered or sprayed, the camera has been knocked out of focus or turned to face somewhere else, or the picture has frozen while the connection stayed up. None of those were visible before: the health monitor asks whether the camera answers, and a covered lens answers fine; the continuity monitor asks whether footage is being written, and footage of a wall is still footage. This is what an attacker arranges before doing anything worth recording. No AI is involved — it is arithmetic over frames already being decoded (edge energy for a blocked view, frame-to-frame difference for a frozen picture, luma-histogram distance for a moved camera). Two decisions make it usable rather than noisy. Blocked-view checks are measured against each camera's OWN rolling median rather than an absolute threshold, so a busy loading bay and a plain corridor both work, and readings taken while a camera is already alerting are excluded from that baseline so a lens left covered cannot quietly become the new normal. And blocked-view checks pause automatically in low light, because a night scene loses detail on its own — without that, every camera in the fleet would report a covered lens at dusk and the feature would be muted by morning. The trade is explicit: a lens covered overnight is reported at first light. Tunable from Settings > Camera health; new metric mymatasan_camera_tamper_total by kind.

### Changed

- **core**: Added docs/FLAGSHIP_HARDENING_PLAN.md, the phased plan closing the 24 findings (F-01..F-24) from the 2026-08-19 mymatasan + myseliasan condition register. Phase 1 (trust the system) covers the myseliasan .selbackup export/restore, a shared audit package plus the missing mymatasan audit log, recording-continuity detection, evidence export with an integrity manifest, tamper/video-loss detection, and a nightly race-detector CI job. Phases 2 and 3 cover fleet-scale operations and competitive feature depth at seam level. The plan's status board is the resume point and is updated in the same commit that lands each work item. Documentation only.

### Fixed

- **core**: CI now runs the Go race detector nightly (and on demand via workflow_dispatch), which nothing in the repo had ever done. The first run found three data races. The real one is in mypintusan's door controller: DoorMachine's fields were unsynchronized across the goroutines that drive it, because Controller.mu only ever guarded the MAP of machines and is released before tickDoors calls Tick — so an operator hitting the lockdown endpoint (apis/doors.go) mutated the same machine the bus goroutine was ticking. Losing a shuntUntil write raises a forced-door alarm on a legitimate entry; losing a contactOpen write means a forced door never alarms at all. DoorMachine now carries its own mutex and takes it in every exported method, returning events only after the lock is dropped so emitDoorEvents cannot re-enter under it. The other two were test-infrastructure races that would have made the new nightly permanently red: myseliasan's fakeSettingsRepo is now mutex-guarded, and infra/safego's captureLogs returns a locked snapshot function instead of a bare pointer to the slice its logger goroutine appends to.
- **myseliasan**: Restoring a myseliasan backup produced a control plane nobody could sign in to. ControlUser.PasswordHash is json:"-" so it never reaches a browser, which also meant the backup bundle's JSON encoding silently dropped it: every local account came back with an empty password. The restore reported success, the fleet CA and the node registry were intact, and the operator was locked out of the result — the worst possible outcome for a feature whose entire purpose is recovering from a lost server. Fixed with a backupUser wrapper that carries the hash across, the same treatment ManagedNode.Token already had for the same reason. Found by actually running a restore end to end: a containerised control plane with two nodes holding real CA-signed certificates, destroyed by deleting both the database and the at-rest encryption key, then restored onto a fresh install with a newly generated key.
