The rest of that bench passed, including the check that matters most — a node certificate issued before the backup completes an mTLS handshake against the restored control channel, while a rogue certificate is rejected.
## 2026-08-19 — mymatasan 1.122.1 (ebe2e60)

### Fixed

- **mymatasan**: Camera credential errors were nearly invisible in the mymatasan UI: `.danger-text` was only ever styled on <button> elements, so the ~25 inline `<p class="field-hint danger-text">` error lines scattered across the app (including a rejected camera login) rendered in the same muted grey as a neutral hint. Errors from the Add Camera dialog, the first-run wizard's add-camera row, and the camera re-auth gate now render through a new shared FormAlert component (warning glyph, red rule, tinted danger-bg panel, role="alert") and the username/password inputs get a red input-error outline, so a wrong password is unmistakable. The Add Camera username field's placeholder also changed from a bare 'admin' (which read as an already-filled value) to a translated 'e.g. admin', and all camera credential placeholders are now italic/dimmed so an example can't be mistaken for a stored value. Separately, double-clicking a live-view tile to maximize it no longer fires when the double-click lands on a control layered over the picture (mute, talk, alert pill, PTZ ring, header buttons); those own their own clicks. The PTZ ring overlay is now pointer-events:none with only its sectors clickable, so the overlay's square corners no longer intercept clicks meant for the tile underneath (maximize and drag-to-reorder work there again).
## 2026-08-17 — myidsan 1.44.1, myiotsan 0.28.0, mymatasan 1.122.0, myseliasan 1.63.1, core 1.90.1 (176c0ec)

### Added

- **core,shared,myseliasan,myidsan,mymatasan,myiotsan**: Adds a suite-wide deployment-mode answer and Phase 1 multi-instance safety. A new shared model (`domain/shared/services/deployment.go`) tiers the five apps into genuinely clusterable (`myseliasan`, `myidsan` -- stateless over Postgres) versus single-instance by design (`mymatasan` owns capture pipelines/local recordings/GPU; `myiotsan` and `mypintusan` each hold a serial port open for the process's lifetime), and exposes a per-app `GET /api/deployment/preflight` readiness checklist (db engine, cache/lock reachability -- pinged, not just name-checked -- the at-rest encryption key's fingerprint for comparing across instances, JWT-secret provenance, per-instance DB pool budget) plus, on the two Tier-A apps, `GET`/`POST /api/deployment/mode` to declare `standalone`/`clustered`. A declared mode turns the existing boot-time shared-state warning from an inference into a stated fact. New `Cipher.Fingerprint()`/`KeyStore.Fingerprint()` (`infra/atrest`) give the at-rest key a comparable, non-secret identity distinct from the per-key-file install marker. Phase 1 also adds real multi-instance safety: `infra/coordination` gains `Locker.TryLock`/`Lock.Valid` and a leader-election primitive (`Elect`/`Leader.IsLeader`) built on them, wired into every app as `apphost.Dependencies.Leader`; myseliasan's notification rollup, retention purges, correlator sweep, dedup-ledger prune, heartbeat reconciliation, and AI digest schedule/purge are now leader-gated so a replicated deployment runs each exactly once instead of once per instance, and myidsan's audit-retention purge is gated the same way (its session gauge is deliberately left ungated, since it only reads and publishes a number to its own Prometheus scrape target). Schema bootstrap is separately serialized across instances starting together via a new deployment-wide migration lock. `transaction.lockProvider` is now editable beside the cache in both Tier-A apps' Settings `storage` section and in myidsan's setup wizard's "Where sessions live" step, so an operator can no longer reach a half-clustered install with a shared cache and a per-process lock. myseliasan's setup wizard gains a new Deployment step (before sign-in and node adoption); both Tier-A apps' Settings panels and the three Tier-B apps' Settings surfaces render the shared readiness checklist (read-only, with a fixed reason, for Tier B).
- **core,myseliasan**: Phase 2 of suite deployment mode: myseliasan gains a node-owner registry and instance-to-instance command forwarding, closing the first of Phase 1's two documented multi-instance gaps and half of the operator-facing pain of the second. A node dials in and holds its control channel open to exactly one instance, so every instance previously knew only its own nodes -- the heartbeat reconciler fell back to an mTLS probe for nodes attached elsewhere and, where that probe could not reach them, falsely marked healthy nodes `lost`; opening a node's own screens (Settings dialog, recording playback) only worked when the browser request happened to land on the owning instance, a 1-in-N coin flip. New `apps/myseliasan/services/node_owner.go` (`NodeOwnerRegistry`) publishes a TTL-leased `nodeowner:<nodeId>` claim to the shared cache on connect (last-writer-wins, since a reconnect elsewhere is genuinely a new owner) and withdraws it on disconnect (new `ControlServer.SetOnDisconnect` hook, fired after the connection is torn down); `registry.SetControlPresence` now consults this registry's `ConnectedAnywhere` instead of the local connection map, fixing the false-`lost` heartbeat bug. New `apps/myseliasan/services/node_peer.go` adds the forwarding hop: a peer credential derived from `jwt.secret` via HKDF-SHA256 (domain-separated from the at-rest boot-log fingerprint so neither can be produced from the other -- no new secret to configure or leak), a `ForwardingSender` that decorates the existing narrow `ControlSender` interface so both the node command proxy and recording-stream playback become cluster-aware without either learning that instances exist, and `POST /api/internal/cluster/node-forward` (mounted outside session auth; only exists when clustering is configured) as the receiving side. New `cluster` config block (`infra/config/config_models.go`'s `ClusterConfigModel`: `advertiseUrl`, `ownershipTtlSeconds` default 30, `forwardTimeoutSeconds` default 30, `insecureSkipVerify`) controls all of this. Live-verified on two instances sharing one Postgres + one Redis with a real adopted mypintusan node: forwarding proven end-to-end (non-owning instance returned the node's real reply, HTTP 200; owning instance never forwarded its own traffic), and the false-lost fix proven with a control experiment (node's mTLS port pointed at a dead port so only the registry could report presence -- node stayed online across 3 heartbeat sweeps with zero false alerts; suppressing the registry claim past the grace window reproduced the `lost` bug on demand, and restoring the claim recovered it).
- **core,myseliasan**: Phase 4 of suite deployment mode: myseliasan now forwards live camera video across instances, closing the last documented multi-instance gap. A node opens TWO independent connections to the control plane -- the control channel for commands (made cluster-aware in Phase 2) and a separate media channel carrying camera RTP -- and nothing guarantees they land on the same instance; only the instance holding the media channel could answer a browser's WebRTC offer for that node's cameras, so behind a load balancer a viewer had a 1-in-N chance of landing somewhere that could serve them, and otherwise got "node media channel is not connected" for a camera that was streaming perfectly. The fix forwards the NEGOTIATION, not the media: a browser's SDP offer for a node held elsewhere is passed to the owning instance (new apps/myseliasan/services/media_peer.go: PeerMediaOfferPath, MediaOfferRequest/Reply, PeerClient.ForwardMediaOffer, PeerMediaOfferHandler, reusing the same derived peer token as the command hop), which builds the answer with its own WebRTC engine; the answer carries that instance's own ICE candidates, so the browser then peers with it DIRECTLY and the video never crosses the load balancer or a second instance -- relaying the RTP itself would have doubled bandwidth and added a hop of latency to every frame, to move bytes already designed to go peer-to-peer. apps/myseliasan/services/node_owner.go now builds a second, independently-namespaced ownership registry (NewMediaOwnerRegistry, key prefix mediaowner: vs the control channel's nodeowner:) so a node's media-channel and control-channel claims are tracked and released completely separately -- sharing one namespace would send a camera offer to an instance holding only the command channel, and vice versa, both failing in a way indistinguishable from the node being down. apps/myseliasan/services/media_relay.go gains MediaRelayHub.SetOwnershipHooks, firing the disconnect hook AFTER the connection is torn down so a peer can never observe it as still held here. apps/myseliasan/apis/node_media.go's offer handler now forwards to the owning instance when the node isn't held locally (authorization already happened on the instance the browser reached, before forwarding) and gains AnswerLocalOffer for the receiving side, which deliberately does not re-authorize -- the forwarding instance already resolved the operator's live session and per-node grant, and re-resolving from a role id carried on the wire would be a weaker check. node_peer.go's bearer-token comparison was extracted into a shared constant-time peerTokenMatches so the command hop and the new media hop can never drift into different comparisons. Also included: fleet-rule EDITS now propagate across instances too -- correlator.Save/Delete each call the new announceRulesChanged after their own Reload succeeds, which publishes on node_events.go's new fleet-rules-changed topic (correlator.SetOnRulesChanged, wired end-to-end in app.go); an operator's rule edit or delete now reloads every instance's rule cache, not just the one that served the request. This is unrelated to, and complements, Phase 3's fleet-rule fix (a rule whose CLAUSES span nodes on different instances correctly arms via the existing event bus, about event correlation) -- this one keeps the rule DEFINITION itself in sync after an edit. The call was, briefly during development, wired everywhere (the callback registered, the subscriber listening, the topic defined) except actually invoked from Save/Delete, and because Go does not flag an unused method that state compiled and passed every other test while doing nothing; it is now guarded by a source-level (go/ast) regression test, correlate_announce_test.go, that parses correlate_crud.go and fails if either call site is ever removed -- a feature whose failure mode is silence needs a test that checks the call was made, not one that infers it from a downstream effect. The setup wizard's deployment caveat list is now down to one item: the three now-closed caveats (node liveness/screens, live notification bell/cross-instance rules, live camera video) were removed from all four locales, and the shared deploy.caveatsTitle/caveatsBody copy was reworded in all four languages from 'Not ready for multiple instances yet' to 'One thing to know first' / a single-remaining-point framing, since an overstated warning is one operators learn to discount.

### Fixed

- **core,myseliasan**: Phase 3 of suite deployment mode: a new fire-and-forget event bus (infra/eventbus, in-process by default, Redis when clustered) fans node events and notifications out to every myseliasan instance, closing the last documented Phase 1 gap -- a fleet rule whose clauses span nodes attached to DIFFERENT instances used to never arm at all (it went quiet rather than firing twice) -- and, as a side effect of the same wiring, makes the live notification feed (SSE) span the deployment too, so a browser connected to any instance now sees everything the deployment raises rather than only what its own instance ingested. apps/myseliasan/services/node_events.go defines two topics deliberately: 'node-events' carries the RAW node event so every instance's correlator sees what the origin's did (never the control plane's own re-published notification, which would let one rule's alert satisfy another rule's clause), and 'notifications' relays EVERY notification an instance publishes -- a node's, but equally a node-lost alert, a certificate-expiry warning, or the daily digest -- to the other instances' live feeds via a new domain/notification.Service.RelayToStream (stream-only, never re-persisted, since the origin already wrote the row). infra/config.ClusterConfigModel gains eventBusProvider, which DEFAULTS TO cache.provider rather than needing its own setting, since a deployment that already pointed the cache at Redis has already answered whether instances have somewhere shared to meet; eventbus.New returns an error on an unrecognised provider rather than silently degrading to in-process, since that would leave a clustered deployment looking configured while delivering to nobody. Live-verified on two Postgres+Redis instances with a real adopted node: killing the node on instance A (which never held its control channel) delivered the 'Node offline' alert on instance B's live SSE feed, carrying the origin's own notification id. Benching this phase also found and fixed two PRE-EXISTING bugs unrelated to clustering itself: (1) the live notification feed died 30 seconds after opening on every install, standalone included, because Go's http.Server.WriteTimeout is an absolute deadline that never resets as more is written -- fixed by extending the response's write deadline (http.NewResponseController) before every write and on a 20s heartbeat, so the stream lives as long as it is being consumed rather than never expiring at all, plus adding the missing Unwrap() method to the request-log middleware's ResponseWriter wrapper, without which ResponseController could not reach the real connection at all and the deadline fix silently did nothing; (2) the fleet's own parent certificate (its cert and private key stored in two separate un-locked rows) could be permanently corrupted when two instances issued at the same moment and their writes interleaved, leaving one instance's certificate beside the other's key -- undetected at write time and persisting forever, since every later boot found both rows non-empty and handed them straight to the mTLS listener, which then refused to start and left every adopted node dark until an operator cleared the rows by hand. Fixed by validating the stored pair (tls.X509KeyPair) on every read and re-issuing on mismatch, and by writing the key before the certificate so a concurrent reader sees either the old matching pair or a key with no certificate yet, never a certificate that looks current beside a key that does not belong to it.
- **core,shared,myseliasan,myidsan**: Review-round fixes to the deployment-mode work, three of which affect standalone installs as much as clustered ones. (1) Distributed lock keys are now namespaced by application: `infra/coordination`'s Redis locker built keys as `<prefix>:tx:<resource>`, so two apps in the suite pointed at one Redis -- the documented and recommended arrangement, since Redis is what makes either app clusterable -- shared a single key space. myseliasan's leader lease and myidsan's leader lease were literally the same key, `kopiv2:tx:lock:leader`, which meant only ONE of the two apps could hold leadership at a time: whichever lost the race silently ran none of its scheduled singletons (audit retention, rollups, reconciliation, digests) for as long as the other app kept renewing. Keys are now `<prefix>:<appName>:tx:<resource>`. (2) A node that reconnects to the SAME instance no longer withdraws its own live ownership claim: the control server's teardown path fired the disconnect hook unconditionally, but a reconnecting node replaces the connection entry first and the OLD goroutine's teardown arrives afterwards, so the claim covering a healthy, connected node was released and the heartbeat -- which reads presence from that registry -- marked it lost and alerted on it. `ControlServer.remove` and the media relay's equivalent now report whether the connection they tore down was still the current one, and only that case announces a disconnect. (3) The bootstrap migration lock's 10-minute wait now actually applies: `Lock()` bounds its wait by the locker's own `WaitTimeout` (30s by default), so the wait was decoration and a migration taking longer than 30 seconds -- exactly the case worth serializing -- made every other instance give up and proceed WITHOUT the lock, into the concurrent DDL the lock exists to prevent. `buildTransactionLocker` accepts a wait override and bootstrap passes the full 10 minutes. (4) The correlator's event observation is leader-gated in the same place the sweep is, so a follower no longer accumulates arming state it will never fire from. (5) The Deployment panel now saves when only the acknowledgment changed, not just the mode -- an install already declared clustered could not record a fresh acknowledgment, so when the caveats changed under an operator, the one action the screen asked for was the one it refused to accept. (6) Event-bus topics are namespaced by application for the same reason and in the same shape (`<prefix>:<appName>:bus:<topic>`): `infra/eventbus` keyed topics by `KeyPrefix` alone, and its own comment claimed that let several apps share one Redis -- but every app in the suite is configured with the SAME prefix, so it did not. No collision exists today, because myseliasan is the only caller; this closes it before a second app picks a topic name in common and one app's events start arriving at the other's subscribers, silently.

## 2026-08-11 — myidsan 1.43.0, myiotsan 0.27.0, mymatasan 1.121.0, myseliasan 1.60.0 (3c73c0b)

### Added

- **mymatasan,myseliasan,myidsan,myiotsan**: Uninstalling now offers a clean wipe on every packaged path, not just an interactive Windows prompt. Linux gained an automated uninstaller: the .deb/.rpm packages install /usr/sbin/<app>-uninstall (one shared script, resolved per package from argv[0]), which drives apt/dnf for you and takes --purge-data to erase /opt/<app>, plus --yes for unattended runs and --dry-run to preview. Underneath it, the postremove scriptlets honour KOPIV2_PURGE_DATA=1, so `sudo KOPIV2_PURGE_DATA=1 apt-get purge <app>` performs the same wipe for config-managed hosts with no helper involved; a wipe also drops the service account. On Windows the uninstaller learned /CLEANDATA and /KEEPDATA switches and the same KOPIV2_PURGE_DATA variable, so a silent uninstall can finally erase the data dir instead of always keeping it. The behaviour is now regression-tested: deploy/nfpm/tests/ builds a throwaway package around each app's real maintainer scripts and drives it through real dpkg/apt, rpm/dnf and a pty, and a new Package scripts check workflow runs that plus ShellCheck on PRs touching deploy/nfpm or the goreleaser configs.
## 2026-08-10 — myseliasan 1.59.0, core 1.87.0 (05bc630)

### Added

- **core,myseliasan**: The myseliasan fleet agent can now answer questions about how the products work, not only about what the fleet is doing. A new app-agnostic retrieval library (domain/shared/manual/retrieval) indexes the suite's built-in manuals in memory -- sections split on the same '## Heading {#anchor}' boundaries the contextual help already deep-links to, ranked with BM25 plus a topic boost for a query term found in a section's heading, title or slug, over a tokenizer built for all four shipped languages: character bigrams for Chinese (which has no word delimiters, so a whitespace split retrieves nothing at all), diacritic/tatweel/alef folding plus light clitic stripping for Arabic (without which a reader's query and the article that answers it can share not one byte), and English plural/verb-form folding so that 'how do I add a camera' reaches the article titled 'Adding cameras'. myseliasan carries BOTH its own manual and mymatasan's, because a control plane is where operators ask questions about software running on other machines; every citation names the product it documents. Ask-the-fleet now appends up to three retrieved sections to its grounding, under a prompt that separates 'what this fleet is doing now' from 'how the software works in general', and the SSE stream gained an 'event: sources' frame carrying which sections the finished answer actually cited -- rendered in the SPA as chips that open the help drawer for this app's own manual and name the product for the other's. A new GET /api/agent/docs returns the same ranked sections with snippets and needs NO language model, so the help half of the assistant works on the default appliance where no model is configured: the chat card now offers manual search instead of only 'the LLM is disabled'.
- **myseliasan**: Nine new articles in myseliasan's built-in user manual, in all four shipped languages (36 files), taking the control plane's manual from 6 articles to 15 per language. Fleet: Managing a node (details are control-plane labels that change nothing on the appliance; working on a node's own screens over the control channel so the browser never touches the node; the certificate and the quiet way a node leaves the fleet when auto-renew is off; releasing vs remotely wiping; why an unrecognized node holding a VALID certificate is worth looking at), The notification feed (unread as a work queue rather than a badge; why the event rows live here while the footage stays on the node that recorded it; how one noisy source ruins a feed), and Fleet rules (correlating events across different nodes; why the 'must NOT have happened' half is what lets a rule express innocence and stops a legitimate badge entry being reported as an intrusion; what window, grace delay and cooldown each answer). Map & sites: The fleet map (placing nodes, the marker legend, and the split between your data and the PMTiles basemap -- so a control plane with no basemap shows a degraded map rather than a broken one, and why downloading a region is exactly the wrong move on a site that is meant to have no egress) and Buildings and floor plans (buildings vs outdoor areas vs point assets, areas named the way staff say them, uploading a plan image without losing walls or camera pins, and why a camera has exactly one pin and cannot be placed twice -- two pins would mean the system believes a device is in two rooms and every location-based answer built on it would quietly be wrong). Reports: the four PDFs, period and site scoping, and an honest account of the one real limitation -- report text uses a built-in Latin-script font, so CJK and Arabic characters in user-entered names render blank, with the practical workaround. AI agent: The fleet digest (findings are computed by ordinary code and appear whether or not a language model is enabled -- the model writes narrative over them and can neither invent nor suppress one), Ask the fleet (the two grounding sources, source citations, single-node drill-down, and what the assistant will not do), and Setting up a language model (off / external endpoint / managed sidecar, air-gapped Import where provenance is recorded rather than enforced, and how every path degrades instead of failing). Twenty of the questions these articles answer are now golden retrieval tests, so the fleet agent can answer for the control plane it runs on instead of only for the node appliances.
- **core,myseliasan**: Three more articles in myseliasan's built-in manual, in all four shipped languages (12 files), taking it to 18 articles per language. Users, roles and access explains the property the page is built on: a new sign-in arrives with NO role and sees an access-pending screen rather than the control plane, because authenticating proves who somebody is and does not say what they may see -- plus disabling rather than deleting a departed colleague, retiring the stock superadmin via the bootstrap handoff, the viewer default for a new role, features-before-paths access, longest-prefix-wins with deny-by-default, and the fact that MENUS AND API PERMISSIONS ARE THE SAME MATRIX (a menu switch grants GET on that section's path, so a role can never see a page it cannot call and hiding a menu is not a measure applied on top of a real one -- it is the real one). Node access is documented as the separate question it is. The audit log covers the append-only record, what each column means, that DENIED attempts are recorded too (a refused action is evidence, and a log of only successes could not tell the two situations apart), System as a genuine non-human actor, and using it to tell a released node from a self-dropped one from one that is merely offline. The settings editor covers save-then-restart and why applying mid-request would change the rules under sessions already in flight, the safe subset (the database, server and bootstrap settings are deliberately NOT editable from the app that needs them to run), blank-means-unchanged for secrets, per-section restore defaults, and config.json as the recovery path when a change stops the app starting. Also fixes a stale UI string in all four languages: the Users page claimed every sign-in is provisioned as a viewer, which contradicts the shipped pending-clearance behaviour the tests assert.
- **myseliasan**: Three more articles in myseliasan's built-in manual, in all four shipped languages (12 files), taking it to 21 articles per language. Troubleshooting collects the failure modes documented across the rest of the manual into one page ordered by what actually happens rather than what is most interesting -- discovery finding nothing (almost always the fleet key), a node showing offline (check the certificate first, because auto-renew left off lets it lapse with nothing breaking loudly), a node that keeps connecting but is not in the list, a fleet rule that never fires (the text not matching beats every other cause), too many alerts, a colleague who cannot see a page, a map with no streets, blank names in a PDF, an assistant that will not answer, a setting that did not take effect, and an event with no clip -- each pointing at the article that covers it. Frequently asked questions answers what people ask before trusting a control plane with a site: whether anything leaves the network (only a map-region or model download, both optional and both with offline alternatives), whether it runs fully air-gapped, what happens to the recorders when the control plane goes down (they keep recording and detecting -- pairing adds central management and moves nothing), where video is actually stored, whether a language model is required, whether the assistant can act, why menus differ between colleagues, why a new sign-in sees nothing, why a camera gets one pin, whether two control planes can manage one node, and why a normal badge entry produced an intrusion alert. Glossary defines the 28 terms the product uses -- absence, adoption, basemap, claim code, control channel, cooldown, digest, finding, fleet key, grace delay, node access, pending, permission matrix, placement, release, self-drop, sidecar, unrecognized node, window, wipe and the rest -- each linked to the article that explains it properly. The manual now cross-links 16 of its own articles across 115 stable anchors, and four more of these questions are golden retrieval tests (32 in total).
- **myseliasan**: One more article in myseliasan's built-in manual, in all four shipped languages (4 files), taking it to 22 articles per language: "Working inside a node" (Fleet section), documenting the embedded node pages opened by the Manage action -- what each kind of node (camera recorder, sensor hub, door controller) exposes, that the browser never talks to the node directly (everything rides the control channel the node dialled outward), live video's WebRTC-then-snapshot-fallback behaviour, and the one property support tends to get asked about: your permission to REACH a node's pages comes from this control plane, but your permission to DO things there is evaluated by the node itself against your node-access grant, so a refusal inside these pages did not come from here. Also documents that an empty page there can mean the node is unreachable rather than genuinely empty, what the remote console escape hatch does and does not tunnel (no streaming endpoints), and the ordered troubleshooting checklist for when a node's pages will not load. Cross-links to "Users, roles and access"'s node-access section.
## 2026-08-09 — myseliasan 1.54.0, core 1.85.0 (ed743f7)

### Added

- **core,myseliasan**: myseliasan (the fleet control plane) adopts the shared, suite-wide built-in user manual that shipped for mymatasan: 6 articles per language (24 files across en/ms/zh/ar) covering what MySeliaSan is, signing in for the first time, the first-run setup wizard, a tour of the workspace, using and printing the manual, and adopting a node -- compiled into the binary so help is always in sync with the running control plane and works with no network access, the posture the whole app (and its myidsan SSO hop) already requires. Served publicly under GET /api/manual, /api/manual/bundle, /api/manual/{slug}, /api/manual/assets/{name}, mounted on the bare router before any auth middleware and deliberately left off the RBAC permission matrix, since the sign-in screen and the first-run wizard are exactly where an operator most needs help and least can authenticate. In the SPA: an ungated Help nav entry (every role that reaches the workspace, since a short menu is exactly the thing a restricted role needs explained) opens the full manual as a workspace tab; a new WorkspaceHeader replaces the bare language/theme strip with a contextual '?' first (TAB_HELP maps the active tab to an article/anchor, falling back to the workspace tour); the sign-in, forced-password-change, and pending-clearance screens each carry a LoginHelpLink; the setup wizard gets a per-step '?' (SETUP_STEP_HELP, one anchor per step); and the Nodes page gets four HelpButtons (fleet key, discovery troubleshooting, releasing, and the claim code inside the adopt dialog). A new manualcheck.UIReferences-driven test (apps/myseliasan/manual/manual_test.go's TestManualUIReferences, currently 19 targets) scans the frontend source and fails the build if a help target names an article slug or heading anchor that does not exist in the shipped manual, catching a renamed-article/dropped-anchor deep-link break before it ships silently.
## 2026-08-09 — mymatasan 1.120.0, core 1.84.0 (1db06e8)

### Added

- **core,mymatasan**: mymatasan ships a built-in, four-language (English/Malay/Chinese/Arabic) user manual compiled into the binary: markdown articles served with no network access, reachable from the sign-in screen, the first-run wizard, and a new Help nav item (every role) plus a contextual '?' help button in the workspace header and on the pre-session screens. Backed by a new shared, app-agnostic library (domain/shared/manual, generalizing myiotsan's single-language kb) that handles language index/bundle/fallback, and a new shared frontend module (@shared/Manual) providing the reader, full-text search, the contextual slide-over drawer, and whole-manual printing (renders every article with a table of contents via the browser print dialog). Six articles ship at launch (welcome, first sign-in, setup wizard, restore from backup, workspace tour, using this manual), each present in all four languages -- enforced by a conformance test (domain/shared/manual/manualcheck) that checks language parity, cross-link resolution, and heading-anchor consistency. Served publicly (no auth) under GET /api/manual, /api/manual/bundle, /api/manual/{slug}, /api/manual/assets/{name} -- deliberately mounted on the public router and kept off the RBAC permission matrix, since it is shipped read-only reference content with no per-user data. This is phase 1 of a suite-wide manual, starting with mymatasan; other apps adopt the same shared library in later phases.
- **core,mymatasan**: Completes mymatasan's built-in user manual: the article set grows from the 6-article phase-1 launch set to 36 articles per language (144 files across en/ms/zh/ar), covering daily use (dashboard, live views, notifications, recordings, object search, people), cameras (adding cameras, camera properties, camera health, ONVIF management), detection (how detection works, detection rules, object classes, teach mode, fire/smoke/plate recognition, training models), recording (configuration, storage and capacity), notification destinations, administration (users and roles, settings reference, encryption at rest, backup and restore, updates and restart, secure wipe and reset, control plane, machine health), and an appendix (troubleshooting, FAQ, glossary). Contextual '?' help buttons (@shared/Manual's HelpButton) are now wired throughout the SPA at the point of use -- every Settings tab, every camera settings tab, the AI detection rules header, the People page and its consent gate, the Recordings header, Object Search, the dashboard anomaly panel, and Notifications -- each deep-linking to the specific manual section that explains it, instead of only the generic workspace-header help from phase 1. A new conformance guard, domain/shared/manual/manualcheck.UIReferences (exercised by apps/mymatasan/manual/manual_test.go's new TestManualUIReferences, currently 42 targets), scans the frontend source for every declared help target and fails the build if a button names an article slug or heading anchor that does not actually exist in the shipped manual -- catching the renamed-article/dropped-anchor class of bug that otherwise breaks a deep link silently (no compile or runtime error, the button just opens the wrong page).
- **core,shared,mymatasan**: Phase 1 of page-level RBAC: a new shared model (`domain/shared/services/page_access.go`) lets an administrator grant a role PAGES at a LEVEL (e.g. "view", "use", "manage", cumulative -- holding a level implies every level before it) instead of editing the raw per-endpoint permission matrix directly. `DerivePermissions` turns a role's held pages into the same `AccessRolePermission` rows the server already enforces, unioning grants up to each held level and applying catalog-declared carve-outs for paths that sit under a broader grant. `IAccessRolePageService` (`role_page.go`) persists what an administrator chose (new `access_role_page` table / `AccessRolePage` entity) and re-derives the role's MANAGED permission rows in one operation, leaving any row an administrator hand-typed under "Advanced" untouched (a new `Managed bool` column on `AccessRolePermission` distinguishes the two), and `AdoptPreset` claims pre-existing unmanaged rows from installs that predate this feature so a one-shot boot adoption cannot duplicate or orphan rows. mymatasan's own catalog (`apps/mymatasan/services/pages.go`: Dashboard, Live Views, Notifications, Recordings, Objects grantable; Cameras, Detection, Teach, People, Settings AdminOnly) is proven to reproduce its existing `Policy()` catalog exactly for the viewer and operator roles, both by matrix diff and by asserting the real authorization matcher decides identically across 20 probe paths -- this is what makes adopting the model for mymatasan's existing roles provably a no-op rather than a silent widening. Building that proof surfaced a real, pre-existing gap: `/api/faces` (face enrollment) had no rule at all in `Policy()` -- correctly denied by default, but invisible in the admin UI that renders the catalog -- so a row was added there too (still no grants; admin-only).

### Security

- **mymatasan,domain**: mymatasan's authorization catalog (`apps/mymatasan/services/rbac.go`'s `Policy()`) had no rule for `GET /api/auth/session`, the first call the SPA makes after a password is accepted to learn the signed-in user's role and must-change-password flag. Because the permission matrix is deny-by-default, that meant the `viewer` and `operator` roles could not complete sign-in at all: the password was accepted and the very next call returned "you do not have permission for this action". Only `admin`/`superadmin` ever worked, because a superadmin role bypasses the matrix entirely and so never exercised this path -- the three-role model was, in practice, a one-role model. Fixed by adding `{Path: "/api/auth/session", Viewer: read, Operator: read}` to the catalog. A second, deeper bug meant that fix alone would not have reached any install whose roles had already been seeded: `domain/shared/services/appliance_rbac.go`'s `EnsureApplianceRoles` skipped a role wholesale the moment it had any permission rows at all, so a rule added to the catalog after first boot was invisible to every existing install and only ever reached brand-new ones. `EnsureApplianceRoles` now backfills instead: a role with zero rows is still seeded in full, but a role that already has rows gets a row added only for a catalog path it has no row for at all, and an existing row's verbs are never touched -- so a site's hand-tuned permissions survive every reboot while a catalog addition still reaches them. Regression-tested at both layers (`apps/mymatasan/services/rbac_test.go`'s `TestPolicy_NonAdminRolesCanCompleteSignIn` walks the App.js sign-in sequence for viewer and operator; the new `domain/shared/services/appliance_rbac_test.go` covers empty-role seeding, never-touch-a-tuned-row, the backfill itself, and idempotency) and live-verified on a throwaway SQLite install, including simulating a pre-fix install by deleting the two session rows and confirming sign-in broke, then watching the backfill restore them on restart.
## 2026-08-08 — myidsan 1.42.1, myiotsan 0.26.0, mymatasan 1.116.1, myseliasan 1.53.1, core 1.80.0 (43f06f2)

### Fixed

- **infra,mymatasan,myidsan,myiotsan,myseliasan**: The shared runtime OpenAPI generator (infra/apidocs) carried a Gorilla-mux route's regex path-parameter constraint straight into the emitted document, e.g. `/api/notifications/{id:[0-9]+}/read` instead of `/api/notifications/{id}/read`. Because the operation's declared path parameter is already named without the constraint, the path segment and its parameter disagreed, which made the whole `/swagger/openapi.json` document invalid to strict consumers -- not just the offending path. OWASP ZAP is the concrete case that surfaced it: it recorded an import error and silently aborted its automation plan before activeScan/report ran, so security scans against any of the apps produced no report at all. `normalizePath` now strips the mux regex constraint via a new `stripPathParamPatterns`/`muxPathParam`, so every emitted path parameter is bare (`{id}`). This is shared infra code served by all five apps' `/swagger` endpoints.
- **mymatasan**: Two mymatasan API handlers leaked an internal-error response for what is really a client-facing 'not found'. DELETE /api/cameras/{id} discarded the id's ParseUint error, so any non-numeric id silently became id 0, sailed past the service, and the repo layer's 'no rows affected' error for that nonexistent row surfaced as a 500 with an internal message; it now validates the id via the existing pathID() helper (400 on a bad id) and confirms the camera exists via GetById before deleting (404 when it does not). GET /api/recording/streams/{cameraId} (listCameraStreams) let StreamOptions's device-lookup miss propagate as a 500 for any unknown cameraId; it now checks camera existence first and returns 404. Found and verified against the OWASP ZAP scan plans in tools/zaproxy.

### Security

- **domain,mymatasan,myiotsan**: The appliance session cookie (`<app>_local_auth`, shared middleware in domain/shared/apis used by both mymatasan and myiotsan) never set the `Secure` cookie attribute, even when the request arrived over TLS. `setLocalAuthCookie`/`SetLocalAuthCookie` now take the inbound `*http.Request` and set `Secure` from the existing `middlewares.IsSecureRequest(r)` helper (true for direct TLS or a trusted proxy's `X-Forwarded-Proto`), so the session cookie can no longer be replayed over a plaintext http:// connection once TLS is in use. It stays conditional rather than always-on because both appliances are legitimately deployed on a LAN over plain HTTP (`server.nonTlsPorts`) -- hardcoding `Secure` would make the browser silently drop the cookie on those installs and every sign-in would appear to succeed and then immediately fail. `local_login_api.go`'s logout now clears the cookie with the same conditional flag so the deleting `Set-Cookie` still matches and actually removes it.
## 2026-08-08 — myseliasan 1.53.0, core 1.79.0 (529c1b8)

### Added

- **infra**: mypintusan (the door access-control appliance) can now be discovered, adopted and managed by a myseliasan control plane, on the identical shared node stack mymatasan and myiotsan already use. domain/shared/fleetnode gains a new node kind, KindDoor. On the node side, a new apps/mypintusan/app/wire_fleet.go wires the pairing service, mTLS enrollment, and a two-channel (no media -- a door controller has no video) node-dialed control channel, plus a new apis/notifications.go exposing the unified event feed (including the ?since= replay endpoint myseliasan's reconnect-replay logic pulls from). Every access decision a controller makes (badge grant, badge deny, operator unlock) now also publishes into that feed via a new NotificationAlarmer.Decision, at Info severity -- this is what lets the fleet correlate a door event against camera and sensor events. Duress is deliberately invisible in that published stream: a duress grant's notification is byte-identical to a normal grant's (the reason is normalised to "ok", a bad PIN or duress PIN both coarsen to "credential-rejected", and Detail/RawCredential are never included); the existing Critical duress alarm, worded for an operator, is unaffected and still fires separately. config.json gains a pairing block (mtlsPort 39532, controlPort 39533, matching the suite-wide port convention; no mediaPort). The Settings screen gains a Fleet panel (fleet key, claim code, status, leave-fleet). RBAC policy gains rows for /api/notifications (viewer-read), /api/notifications/*/read (operator-write) and /api/pairing (admin-only, since joining/leaving a fleet changes who can manage a building's doors remotely). Live-bench verified end-to-end: UDP discovery, claim-code adopt, mTLS enrollment, the control channel, an embedded management UI over the tunnel, a badge decision reaching the parent's unified feed, and a remote unlock issued through the tunnel audited on the node as "cp:admin".
- **myseliasan**: myseliasan's control plane now discovers, adopts and manages mypintusan door-controller nodes (node kind "door") alongside its existing mymatasan camera nodes and myiotsan sensor-hub nodes. The frontend gains tri-state node-kind handling (nodeKindOf/isDoorNode/isCameraNode in layout.js) and a new embedded management surface -- components/nodedoor/ -- that mirrors mypintusan's own real Doors/People/Activity/Readers/Settings pages inside myseliasan's shell, driven over the command tunnel via a new @mypintusan webpack alias and scoped CSS, exactly like the existing nodecam/nodeiot embeds (the node's own fleet-pairing panel is deliberately excluded from the embed, since unpairing from inside the tunnel would sever the tunnel it runs over). Door nodes get their own pill/chip styling, appear correctly in the nodes list, node settings dialog (details-only, same as a sensor hub), fleet-rule kind list/labels/chips, and the fleet map's inspector/asset browser/building editor; a latent bug where the fleet-map node popup fetched /proxy/api/cameras for non-camera nodes (guaranteed 404, noisy node log) is fixed for both existing kinds as a side effect. Fleet rules can now be scoped to a door node kind, and the correlator's flagship cross-domain rule (motion + door opening + no badge) is verified end-to-end against a real door node: a badge accepted (category access.granted) disarms an armed rule within its grace window, a badge denied does not, and a door-scoped absence clause ignores an access.granted-shaped event from a different node kind. Severity heuristics for bare (non-notification) node event frames now also recognize the door-alarm vocabulary (duress/forced/tamper) as Critical.
- **infra**: mypintusan gains the groups/schedules/grants surface that was the missing half of access control: until now a person created through the first-run wizard could not badge through any door, because the fail-closed decision path requires a Grant and nothing served one. A new apps/mypintusan/apis/access_rules.go exposes GET/POST /api/groups (+ /api/groups/{id}/members add/remove, with holder names hydrated so the screen never shows a raw id), GET/POST /api/schedules with embedded weekly windows (rejects a schedule that is neither 24/7 nor has any window, since it would match nothing; EndMin<=StartMin is explicitly valid and means an overnight/night-shift wrap) plus /api/schedules/holidays CRUD, and GET/POST /api/grants (validates all three legs exist before writing, treats a duplicate grant as a no-op returning the existing row). All writes are admin-only on top of the existing deny-by-default RBAC matrix, mirroring doors.create. Deleting a group or schedule still referenced by a grant is refused in plain words; deleting a group cascades its memberships. Every grant/membership mutation now publishes a named access.rule-change notification to the local feed (and, on an adopted node, up the fleet control channel), directly answering the RBAC doc's warning that a grant edit otherwise 'looks like nothing in a log'. The frontend gains an admin-only Access screen (views/Access.js, lib/access.js) and the first-run wizard now calls ensureDefaultAccess after creating the first person: it builds (or reuses, by name) an Everyone group and an Always 24/7 schedule, adds the person, and grants the group the door the wizard just created, so a fresh install badges successfully the moment setup finishes. Live-bench verified: existing grant -> badge GRANTED; DELETE grant -> next badge DENIED (no-grant); POST grant -> next badge GRANTED again, with named notifications for both mutations; a full headless first-run wizard walk produced the Everyone group, membership and door grant, and reused the pre-existing Always schedule on a second run.
## 2026-08-06 — mymatasan 1.115.0, myseliasan 1.52.0 (7bb3532)

### Changed

- **myseliasan,mymatasan**: Phase 2 of the myseliasan fleet AI agent (phase 1: PR #158). Adds an OPTIONAL weekly digest cadence (agent.digest.weeklyEnabled, default false; agent.digest.weekday, default Monday) that runs alongside the daily one on its own persisted guard and always uses a fixed 7-day findings window regardless of the configured daily look-back. The Fleet Health and range-scoped Incident PDF reports now open with an AI Executive Summary built from the same deterministic narrator as the digest (plus an optional English-only LLM paragraph, since the report renderer cannot show non-Latin glyphs) via a new DigestService.GenerateBriefing that persists nothing; a briefing failure or a disabled/unreachable model costs only the section. A fired fleet rule's notification now carries a second, deterministic line of recurrence context ("also fired N times in the last 7 days") computed under a hard 2-second timeout inside the alert path itself (Correlator.SetEnricher) -- never an LLM call. The digest gained a new agent-suggested-rule finding: recurring after-hours activity (22:00-06:00, >=2 events/night on >=3 distinct nights) from one node+category that no existing fleet rule covers surfaces with a "Create rule" button that pre-fills (never auto-saves) the Fleet Rules editor. The events-over-time expected-activity band (GET /api/notifications/baseline) can now narrow to one node's own learned baseline via ?nodeId=, backing new node_baseline_spike/node_baseline_quiet digest findings, via a new Source column folded into the shared notification_rollup table (mymatasan and myseliasan both fold rollups). "Ask the fleet" chat can now drill into exactly one named adopted node's own recent events over the control tunnel (5s cap; an offline node answers honestly as unreachable rather than stalling the reply) instead of central-only grounding. The sidecar LLM gained a "large" model tier (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct Q4_K_M, ~4.7GB) alongside the existing 1.5B default, selectable per-install via POST /api/agent/llm/install/model {tier}, with the operator's choice persisted across restarts via an on-disk marker.
## 2026-08-06 — myseliasan 1.51.0 (8cf8680)

### Changed

- **myseliasan**: Added a fleet AI agent to myseliasan: a deterministic daily "fleet digest" (typed findings computed in plain Go from event volume/baseline/fleet-health/audit signals, localized client-side into en/ms/zh/ar; scheduled at a configurable local hour, default 07:00, plus generate-now) and an optional "ask the fleet" chat (POST /api/agent/chat, SSE-streamed, strictly grounded on a <=8KiB central-data bundle citing notification/node ids). The language model is off by default (agent.llm.mode) and never in a critical path: findings and the feed entry are always produced by the deterministic narrator, and an LLM that is off, unreachable, crashed, or timing out only costs the optional prose narrative. Two LLM modes beyond off: an operator's own OpenAI-compatible endpoint ("external"), or a supervised llama.cpp llama-server child process on loopback ("sidecar", port 49540, pinned release b10289 + Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct Q4_K_M, SHA-256-verified downloads) that can also be provisioned by importing an operator-supplied build/model through the existing file picker for air-gapped sites. New stdlib-only infra/llm OpenAI-compatible chat client backs both modes. This also brought myseliasan's notification analytics substrate up to parity with mymatasan for the first time: hourly rollups (domain.NotificationRollup), a config-driven retention purge for the feed, and a new GET /api/notifications/baseline endpoint (fleet-wide only — relayed camera ids collide across nodes, so no per-camera narrowing here), all of which the digest's baseline findings and the Insight page's chart now read. New settings section "agent" in the safe-subset config editor, a new AI Insight nav page, RBAC feature toggle for /api/agent (viewers get GET by default), new myseliasan_agent_* and myseliasan_notifications_purged_total metrics, and new agent.* audit actions.
## 2026-08-05 — core 1.77.0 (0a6a4c7)

### Added

- **infra**: Added a frontend, a first-run setup wizard, and database-backed runtime settings for the proposed mypintusan (physical access control) app, previously API-only. A new React SPA (apps/mypintusan/views/react-webpack/, building to apps/mypintusan/static/) scaffolded from myidsan's SPA onto the shared frontend/shared (@shared) module covers Doors (cards, remote unlock, lockdown), People (holders + badge issue/revoke), Activity (the access log), Readers, and Settings; apps/mypintusan/app/app.go gained RegisterWebRoutes to serve the SPA shell. src/views/Wizard.js is the first-run wizard (welcome -> site/timezone -> reader cable -> door -> first person + badge -> done), gated on GET /api/setup/state and user.isAdmin. Its door step is the first caller of a new POST /api/doors (apps/mypintusan/apis/doors.go), which creates a door and its entry reader in one call, rolling the door back if the reader insert fails, so a door and reader are never left half-configured. config.json's access/buses block is now a first-boot seed only: a new services/runtime_settings.go (AccessSettings, ported from mymatasan's runtime-settings pattern at the user's direction) persists timezone/tick/PIN-window/offline and the bus/reader/SCBK inventory in the shared runtime_setting table, exposed via a new GET/PUT /api/settings/access + POST /api/settings/access/reset (apps/mypintusan/apis/settings.go); the app boots by reading the live database row, not config.json, and refuses to start on a bad timezone the same way it always did. Driving the SPA live in a real browser (not just unit tests) found and fixed three bugs before this landed: the shared lib/api.js's apiRequest already JSON.stringifies the request body, and the app's own send() helper stringified it a second time, so every write in the SPA failed to unmarshal on the server; the first-run wizard renders instead of the app shell so ToastStack was never mounted while it was up, making every error inside it invisible until an inline error banner was added; and the shared DataTable's columns are {key, label, render} with render(value, row), not title/render(row), and getting that wrong crashed and unmounted the whole app mid-session. docs/MYPINTUSAN_DATA_MODEL.md, README.md and docs/TECHNICAL_SPEC.md are updated to drop the now-stale 'API only, no frontend' framing, and the relevant docs/modules/apps/mypintusan/**.go.md files are updated or added to match.
## 2026-08-04 — core 1.76.0 (539d617)

### Added

- **infra**: Added a new infra/access/osdp package: a pure-Go implementation of the SIA OSDP v2.x wire protocol in CP (Control Panel) mode, plus tools/osdp-sim, a TCP-based OSDP reader (PD) simulator with 17 fault-injection scenarios. This is build-order steps 1-4 of the 6-step plan in docs/MYPINTUSAN_OSDP_PLAN.md for the proposed mypintusan (physical access control) app: framing (crc.go, frame.go, codes.go), a shared PD decoder used by both the simulator and the driver's tests (pd.go), the CP-side polling/state machine and Secure Channel session management (cp.go, bus.go), a TCP transport (transport.go, with serial deferred to step 5), and an OSDP Secure Channel implementation (securechannel.go: AES-128 handshake, per-packet MAC, payload encryption). The design goes through a real fail-closed posture end to end: a door reader that requires Secure Channel and cannot establish or loses it goes out of service and alarms rather than ever falling back to cleartext. No new Go dependencies. There is still no apps/mypintusan and zero application code -- this is infrastructure and a developer tool only, with no consumer yet.
- **infra**: Added the P1 access-control logic layer for the proposed mypintusan (physical access control) app under a new apps/mypintusan package: entities (Holder, Credential, Door, Reader/ReaderProfile, AccessGroup/AccessGroupMember/Schedule/ScheduleWindow/Holiday/Grant, AccessEvent) and services (a pure Decide function implementing the ten ordered gates from docs/MYPINTUSAN_DATA_MODEL.md SS5.1 -- reader health/Secure Channel, lockdown, credential/holder validity, PIN/duress, grant/schedule/holiday, anti-passback, offline-cache policy; Wiegand 26/34-bit decode+encode with parity checking; a Controller that joins the infra/access/osdp bus to the decision path end to end -- decode, resolve, decide, actuate, log, plus lockdown and keypad-then-card PIN pairing; and an injected-clock DoorMachine covering locked/unlocked, door-forced, held-open, and scheduled free-access with first-person-in). 87.3% test coverage, including an end-to-end test against the real OSDP driver and a simulated reader. This is a logic library only: there is no database layer (Store is an interface with only an in-memory test fake -- nothing persists across a restart, no repository/migration/dbsql registration), no apps/mypintusan/apis, no app/ composition root, no config.json, no firstrun/setup wizard, and no frontend, so the app cannot be started. Known gaps stated rather than hidden: Controller.ContactChanged (the myiotsan door-contact seam) is never called by anything; Door.RelayDeviceKey -> myiotsan CommandService.Issue actuation is not implemented (the only shipped Actuator drives a reader's own output); PIN pairing is PIN-then-card only, not card-then-PIN; and Snapshot.AntiPassbackViolation is an input nothing computes (a P3 feature). docs/MYPINTUSAN_DATA_MODEL.md, docs/MYPINTUSAN_HARDWARE_PLAN.md and docs/MYPINTUSAN_OSDP_PLAN.md are updated to reflect this partially-built P1 status.
- **infra**: Added the persistence layer for the proposed mypintusan (physical access control) app: apps/mypintusan/services/store_sql.go's SQLStore, a database-backed implementation of the existing Store interface built on the shared dbsql.NewGenericRepo[T] (one repo per entity -- readers, doors, credentials, holders, group memberships, grants, schedules, schedule windows, holidays, access events, access groups), with var _ Store = (*SQLStore)(nil) pinning it interchangeable with the in-memory test fake. Every child lookup (a holder's grants, a schedule's windows) deliberately goes through Get with an explicit Equal/In filter rather than dbsql.GetByForeign, because SelectByForeign still hardcodes limit=1 and would silently drop all but one child row; regression tests seed 2+ children and assert all of them come back. store_sql_test.go runs the identical end-to-end scenario from controller_test.go (badge on a simulated OSDP reader -> decision -> strike -> access event) against a REAL SQLite database via bootstrap.Ensure, not mocks, and also defines Entities() -- the 12-struct schema list this app owns, for a future app/ composition root to hand to bootstrap.Ensure. controller_test.go was refactored (newRig -> newRigWithStore + seedDefaults) so that same scenario runs over both stores. Along the way, found and documented a second data-layer inconsistency: dbsql.GetById does not return (nil, nil) for a missing row the way its own code comment claims -- SelectById's underlying Select turns an empty result set into an error ('no result found') rather than a nil map, so GetById propagates that as a wrapped error instead of a clean not-found. GetByUnique's identical-looking nil check is NOT affected (SelectByUnique genuinely returns a nil map there). The store's local isNotFound helper works around this; the misleading comment in infra/db/sql/generic_repo.go and its module doc were corrected to describe the real behavior, with no change to GetById's actual return values or error wrapping.
- **infra**: Wired the previously test-only mypintusan (physical access control) logic and persistence layers into a runnable application for the first time: apps/mypintusan/app/app.go (the apphost.App composition root -- identity/local-admin seeding, login guard, RBAC role seeding, the SQLStore, the alarm sink routing door alarms into the shared notification feed, and the HTTP API), apps/mypintusan/app/runtime.go (supervises one OSDP bus per configured RS-485/TCP segment, re-dialling with 1s-30s backoff whenever the transport dies, and holds site lockdown state outside any single controller so a bus reconnect can never unseal a building), apps/mypintusan/apis (doors/readers, holders/credential issue-revoke, the read-only access log, lockdown, and the shared setup-wizard endpoints), apps/mypintusan/config + config.json (the site timezone -- required to parse, refuses to boot on a bad zone rather than silently falling back to UTC -- tick interval, PIN pairing window, and the RS-485 bus/reader list with per-reader SCBK), and cmd/mypintusan/main.go, now registered in the root -app launcher. Two production bugs were found and fixed by booting the binary against tools/osdp-sim, neither caught by any existing unit test: (1) infra/access/osdp/bus.go's Bus.Run polled a dead transport forever instead of returning when the port died (a USB-RS485 adapter unplugged, or a gateway reboot, silently killed every door on that segment until a process restart) -- fixed with a one-shot failPort/portErr signal that makes Run return so its owner can re-dial; (2) services.BusActuator was driving the OSDP PD address off Door.RelayChannel, which is actually the relay OUTPUT channel on that device, a different number -- a badge decision granted, the audit row said ok, and the door never opened; fixed by replacing BusActuator's separate Output/Address fields with a single StrikeResolver (services.SQLStore.StrikeFor), which resolves the PD address from the door's entry reader instead. Live-verified end to end against the simulator: 23 tables created, roles and the local admin seeded, the configured bus dialled, 191 granted badge events, a strike fired; the full HTTP surface (login, doors, readers, holders, events, unlock, lockdown) exercised; an operator remote-unlock lands in the same access log as a badge (raw_credential='operator'), and an unlock attempted during lockdown was refused and logged.
## 2026-08-03 — myidsan 1.42.0, myiotsan 0.25.0, myseliasan 1.50.0, core 1.72.0 (e536200)

### Added

- **shared,myseliasan,myidsan,myiotsan**: Added a factory reset ("Reset to factory settings") to myseliasan, myidsan and myiotsan, none of which had one before (mymatasan already did). A new shared domain/shared/services.SystemResetService + domain/shared/apis.SystemResetHandlers/NewResetGate seam gives all three a GitHub-style typed-confirmation reset: stop background services, crypto-erase the at-rest key, erase the app's data directories, close the DB pool, drop/rebuild/reseed the database (bootstrap.Reset), then restart into first-run setup. The confirmation phrase (the app's own name) is verified server-side, not just in the browser, and a live run showed the progress endpoint going unreachable the moment the DB pool closed, so NewResetGate now also serves /system/reset/progress itself (no secrets in that payload) while 503-ing everything else during a reset. Per app: myseliasan erases floor plans/basemap/file storage and crypto-erases the fleet secret key (adopted nodes are not notified and must be re-adopted); myidsan erases uploads and crypto-erases its secret key (wiping it signs every relying app's sessions out, and those apps are not notified); myiotsan erases file storage and the whole at-rest key directory rather than going through KeyStore, since its cipher only exists inside the fleet/pairing block (removing the directory takes the key's .init marker with it, which is what makes the next boot read as a clean first run instead of tripping the fail-closed recovery gate). A shared frontend/shared/src/FactoryReset.js (Danger Zone section + typed-confirmation dialog + blocking progress overlay) is mounted in each app's Settings > System tab, with 21 new reset.* keys added to the shared i18n dictionary in all four locales (en/ms/zh/ar).
## 2026-08-03 — myidsan 1.41.1 (ba757e8)

### Added

- **myidsan**: myidsan gains an in-app config editor over a safe subset of config.json — localAuth, sso, security, storage, logging — the last of the four apps to get one, ported and adapted from myseliasan's settings feature. GET /api/settings, GET/PUT /api/settings/{section}, POST /api/settings/{section}/reset, and POST /api/settings/cache/test are superadmin-only (enforced as route-group middleware, matching audit/backup/mfa-admin), and a save writes the changed leaves back into config.json (every other block preserved byte-for-byte) and reports needsRestart. The security section is the standout: it surfaces the lockout, password-policy, and MFA-policy blocks through their resolved Effective*() values, so an operator can finally see what those absent-from-config blocks resolve to instead of a misleading zero/off reading, with validation floors (maxAttempts >= 2 once lockout is on, minLength >= 8, a known mfa.policy value); mfa.requiredRoleIds is shown read-only because the save path never writes it. audit.retention is deliberately not exposed, and neither are kerberos, login.oidc[]/social providers, or the new webauthn block. Unlike myseliasan's port, there is no server-side file-browse endpoint, since myidsan is the identity provider and the highest-value target in the suite; the sso section only exposes the provider (issuing) side of the shared config block, and authCodeTtlSeconds is capped at 600 seconds. Saves/resets are recorded in the audit trail (section and actor only, never values). The React Settings page has now shipped as a tabbed editor (one tab per section plus a System tab) of grouped cards with per-field (i) info tips, masked secrets (blank keeps the stored value), a dirty-gated Save, per-section Restore-defaults, and a persistent "restart required" banner. A companion POST /api/system/restart endpoint (superadmin-gated middleware, never delegable through the permission matrix) relaunches the process so that banner's Restart button actually does something — without it the page would name a required action it had no way to perform. Verified live: all 6 tabs render, save/validate/reset exercised against a running server, the audit entry confirmed, zero console errors.
- **myidsan**: myidsan gains FIDO2/WebAuthn security keys as a second factor alongside TOTP: a user may hold either or both, and the login gate accepts whichever is presented. New infra/webauthn wraps github.com/go-webauthn/webauthn (new dependency) to resolve the Relying Party ID and allowed origin from the incoming request when unconfigured (host with port stripped, scheme from r.TLS, never from the client's Origin header), requests 'none' attestation, and does not require a resident/discoverable credential so older hardware keys with limited slots still work. Self-service enrol/list/rename/delete lives on the Profile page's new 'Security keys' card (GET/POST/PUT/DELETE /api/mfa/webauthn); a superadmin can clear another account's keys entirely at DELETE /api/mfa-admin/{id}/webauthn, gated by the same step-up re-authentication as a TOTP admin-reset. On the sign-in side, the pre-session MFA challenge now ORs both factor kinds and reports which ones a specific account holds via a new mfaMethods field, so the login screen prompts for a code, a key, or a choice rather than assuming TOTP; two new PUBLIC legs (POST /api/login/mfa/webauthn/begin and /finish) run the two-round-trip assertion ceremony while preserving the existing invariant that no session cookie is issued until the second factor is proven. The identical challenge now also renders on the server-rendered /api/auth/login page (a 'Use a security key' button driven by inline vanilla JS calling the same two endpoints), which is where every relying app's SSO hop lands, myseliasan included. Building that page's support caught and fixed a real bypass introduced by shipping WebAuthn: that handler's MFA challenger had been wired with the TOTP service only, so an account whose ONLY enrolled factor was a security key would have cleared that page's gate with no second factor checked at all, on every relying app in the suite. Account deletion now also removes an account's TOTP factor and every enrolled security key (previously TOTP rows were harmlessly orphaned, but a WebAuthn credential id is unique across the whole table, so an orphaned row would have silently refused to let that same physical key be re-enrolled to a recreated account later). A signature counter that fails to advance is the spec's documented clone signal, but most platform authenticators and every synced passkey legitimately report 0 forever, so that ambiguous case is accepted rather than refused, flagged on the credential row, and written to the audit trail (webauthn.clone_warning) instead of locking out working hardware. Backup & restore now carries enrolled security keys inside the existing 'mfa' section; the first draft of that leg held the raw entity and silently dropped the public key on marshal (PublicKey is json:"-"), which would have restored cleanly and then failed every assertion — fixed with a backupWebauthnCredential wrapper, covered by a regression test. Verified end-to-end using Chrome's CDP virtual authenticator (real key-pair, real attestation/assertion signatures checked server-side): enrol from Profile, sign out, password sign-in, challenge advertises mfaMethods, complete with the key, session issued; and separately on the server-rendered login page, confirming the key button appears with no code field for a key-only account. The sign-counter/clone-warning branch (unreachable without real hardware) and Active Directory/Samba AD account interplay specifically are not yet exercised.

### Fixed

- **myidsan**: Fixes the side-nav rail overflowing the viewport once the Settings menu item was added: the nav is a 100vh flex column of brand/nav/foot, and the nav list had no overflow handling, so the extra item pushed the bottom entries (and the Theme toggle / Sign out / session line in the foot) off-panel and unreachable — the page grew a document scrollbar instead, which never helped because the rail is position:sticky at 100vh. The nav now scrolls internally (flex: 1 1 auto + min-height: 0 + overflow-y: auto, with the brand and foot pinned via flex-shrink: 0) with a slim overlay-style scrollbar that stays invisible until hovered/focused. A same-day follow-up commit corrected the fix's own claim that the other three apps lacked this rule — they already had a more complete version of it that a shallow grep missed — and instead brought myidsan's styles.css into line with the shared rbac-standard.css treatment (same --nav-scroll/--nav-scroll-hover tokens, same overlay-scrollbar shape) rather than leaving a third variant of the rule in the codebase.
## 2026-08-02 — myidsan 1.39.0, myiotsan 0.24.0, mymatasan 1.114.0, myseliasan 1.49.0, core 1.71.0 (bc0a2ad)

### Added

- **core,shared,mymatasan,myidsan,myseliasan,myiotsan**: Extracted the first-run setup-wizard completion flag (previously near-identical copy-pasted services in mymatasan and myidsan) into one canonical domain/shared/services.ISetupStateService and domain/shared/apis.SetupHandlers pair, then used that seam to bring every app in the suite up to wizard parity: myseliasan gained a first-run wizard it never had (welcome, import a myidsan SSO bundle and save it, first site, adopt a node, handover to a real superadmin, done), myiotsan's onboarding wizard was promoted off a per-browser localStorage flag onto the same server-side setup.state row so dismissal now sticks per install rather than per browser (and its enrol/ready steps now show live enrollment-window and quarantine-candidate state instead of describing them abstractly), and myidsan's wizard gained a 4th step, "Where sessions live", that reads, live-tests, and saves the storage.cache settings section in place. The shared service also picked up myidsan's stricter insert guard (row == nil || row.Id == 0) as the canonical behavior, since mymatasan's original copy lacked it and would have silently persisted nothing against a repo that signals "missing" via a zero-value row rather than an error.
## 2026-07-30 — myidsan 1.38.0, myiotsan 0.23.0, mymatasan 1.113.0, myseliasan 1.48.0, core 1.70.0 (75f7245)

### Added

- **core,infra,mymatasan,myidsan,myiotsan,myseliasan**: Closes the remaining half of the myidsan productization plan's Phase 4 resource-limits item (4.3): runtime log rotation was previously by calendar day only, so one chatty or hostile day could fill the disk long before logging.cleanup.maxRetentionDays -- which deletes whole days -- became relevant. infra/logging now supports a per-file size cap: a new logging.maxFileSizeMb config key (0/negative = uncapped, the historic behaviour, wired through infra/config and infra/apphost's buildRuntimeLogger) rolls the active file to <name>-<date>.<n>.log once the cap is hit, resuming at the highest unfilled sequence on restart rather than reopening and overflowing an already-full file. Because retention discovers deletable files through logFiles() and dateValueFromPath(), both were changed in the same commit so a rotated file is never left undeletable: logFiles() gained a second glob for the sequenced shape, and dateValueFromPath() strips a suffix from the date component only when that suffix is entirely digits, so a lookalike name such as <name>-<date>.bak.log is still rejected rather than swept up and deleted. This is mutation-guarded by a new TestSizeRotatedFilesAreStillPruned (reverting only the parser half makes it delete 1 of 3 seeded files instead of 3). All four shipped deploy/dist/*-config.json now default logging.maxFileSizeMb to 100, since an uncapped default is what created this gap. Found and fixed in passing while touching this file: the runtime-log viewer's newest-first ordering (fileLogger.List) tie-broke same-second entries by string-comparing the RFC3339 Time field, which is wrong on two counts -- Go trims trailing fraction zeros so '.1Z' can string-compare greater than '.1000001Z', and on Windows the clock is coarse enough that adjacent writes get a byte-identical Time, leaving ties unresolved so the stable sort silently returned them oldest-first. This made TestFileLoggerWritesAndListsNewestFirst fail on Windows roughly 90% of the time (37/40 measured runs) while passing on Linux CI, which is why it went unnoticed. Ordering is now derived from append (read) order alone, needs no clock, and cannot tie (0/40 failures after the fix).
- **myidsan**: myidsan gains an in-app config editor over a safe subset of config.json — localAuth, sso, security, storage, logging — the last of the four apps to get one, ported and adapted from myseliasan's settings feature. This change is backend/API only: GET /api/settings, GET/PUT /api/settings/{section}, POST /api/settings/{section}/reset, and POST /api/settings/cache/test are superadmin-only (enforced as route-group middleware, matching audit/backup/mfa-admin), and a save writes the changed leaves back into config.json (every other block preserved byte-for-byte) and reports needsRestart. The security section is the standout: it surfaces the lockout, password-policy, and MFA-policy blocks through their resolved Effective*() values, so an operator can finally see what those absent-from-config blocks resolve to instead of a misleading zero/off reading, with validation floors (maxAttempts >= 2 once lockout is on, minLength >= 8, a known mfa.policy value). audit.retention is deliberately not exposed — the audit trail's only removal path stays config-file-only on purpose, so the superadmin whose actions it records cannot reach the knob from inside the product — and neither are kerberos or login.oidc[]/social providers. Unlike myseliasan's port, there is no server-side file-browse endpoint, since myidsan is the identity provider and the highest-value target in the suite; the sso section only exposes the provider (issuing) side of the shared config block, and authCodeTtlSeconds is capped at 600 seconds. Saves/resets are recorded in the audit trail (section and actor only, never values). The React Settings page itself is not part of this change and remains outstanding.
## 2026-07-30 — core 1.69.1 (11331ed)

### Changed

- **core,docs**: Closes §4.5 of docs/MYIDSAN_PRODUCTIZATION_PLAN.md ("ZAP scan and k6 load profile"): myidsan was the only one of the four apps with neither an OWASP ZAP plan nor a k6 load profile, despite being the identity provider every other app's login depends on. No Go code changed -- this is dev tooling plus documentation. Adds three ZAP Automation Framework plans (tools/zaproxy/plans/myidsan-{baseline,api,full}.yaml, modelled on the myseliasan equivalents: JSON login at POST /api/login/default, double-submit CSRF via the existing csrf-doublesubmit.js script) and three k6 scripts (tools/k6/scripts/myidsan-{smoke,load,stress}.js), each with a matching config/myidsan.target.env.example, and wires -App myidsan / --app myidsan into tools/zaproxy/scan.ps1|scan.sh and tools/k6/run.ps1|run.sh (default target https://host.docker.internal:3001). tools/k6/scripts/lib/session.js gained a new exported loginFresh(loginPath): unlike the existing ensureLogin (once-per-VU on purpose, so a read test pays bcrypt once), loginFresh performs an unconditional, uncached login and does not fail() on a bad status, because in a stress run at the knee the failures are the measurement -- it exists to isolate the identity provider's LOGIN throughput from its read throughput, which sit an order of magnitude apart (a login storm forces every request through bcrypt; a read-load test only bcrypts once per VU). myidsan-stress.js exposes this as MODE=login (default read). Two IdP-specific hazards, absent from the other three apps' tooling, are carried into the plans/configs/READMEs: (1) exclusions have to cover 'would break the scanner itself' (session revoke, MFA clear, password change, account disable, RBAC-matrix mutation), not just 'destructive' (client-secret rotation, SSO-CA regen), while deliberately leaving /api/auth/authorize, /api/auth/token, the login endpoints, and MFA verification in scope since that is the actual point of scanning an IdP; (2) the failed-login lockout (per IP and per account) must be disabled on the throwaway target before an active scan or a MODE=login run, in addition to the rate limiter, or the run measures the lockout instead of the app. Updated tools/zaproxy/README.md and tools/k6/README.md (both previously documented only mymatasan/myseliasan/myiotsan) with a myidsan section apiece, matching table/file-list entries, and a Files-list correction noting csrf-doublesubmit.js is now shared by myseliasan and myidsan. docs/HOWTO.md and the other supporting specs do not catalogue this per-app tooling today (verified by search) so were left untouched.
## 2026-07-30 — myidsan 1.36.0 (f5f16c4)

### Added

- **myidsan**: Closes §4.4 (IdP metrics) of the myidsan productization plan: myidsan had only two app-specific Prometheus series and was the only one of the four apps absent from docs/HOWTO.md's metrics catalogue. Four new metrics follow the suite's rule of instrumenting what fails silently, which for an identity provider mostly means failures somebody else experiences. myidsan_token_exchange_total{outcome} counts every authorization-code redemption at POST /api/auth/token across a closed set of nine outcomes (success, bad_request, unsupported_grant, client_unknown, secret_invalid, redirect_invalid, code_invalid, code_mismatch, server_error); a non-success is a relying app that just failed to sign a user in, and nothing on the myidsan side otherwise raises anything an operator would notice. myidsan_audit_write_failures_total counts audit entries that could not be persisted -- the audit service swallows that write error on purpose so it can never fail the action being audited, which means a trail that has stopped recording has no other symptom. myidsan_audit_retention_purged_total counts rows removed by the existing age-based retention purge, so trail shrinkage is attributable. myidsan_sessions_active is a gauge, polled once a minute by a new scheduler task rather than incremented at issue/revoke time (a session can also end on its own via cache expiry, which a hand-maintained counter would never notice), backed by a new ISessionService.CountActive that is verified against a real SQLite database rather than the package's fake repo because it filters on a boolean column that the query builder renders as a quoted literal. Wiring: federatedAuthApi.token records the new token-exchange counter on every path out of the handler; auditService gained an optional WithMetrics(...) attach point used from app.go; NewFederatedAuthApi gained a trailing metrics parameter. Updated docs/HOWTO.md's metrics catalogue, docs/MYIDSAN_PRODUCTIZATION_PLAN.md §4.4 (marked DONE, with upstream LDAP/OIDC latency and SSO CA expiry explicitly left for later), docs/TECHNICAL_SPEC.md's Application Metrics Principle section, the root README's Telemetry section, and added a Runtime Metrics section to apps/myidsan/README.md matching the other three apps.
## 2026-07-29 — myidsan 1.35.1, myiotsan 0.22.0, mymatasan 1.112.0, myseliasan 1.47.0, core 1.69.0 (bcc3e0d)

### Added
